Every conformal net has an associated unitary VOA
André G. Henriques, James E. Tener

Abstract
Unitary vertex operator algebras (VOAs) and conformal nets are the two most prominent mathematical axiomatizations of two-dimensional unitary chiral conformal field theories. They are conjectured to be equivalent, but a rigorous comparison has proven challenging. We resolve one direction of the conjecture by showing that every conformal net has an associated unitary VOA.



1
Introduction

There currently exist two main, well-developed, mathematical formalisms for
2d
unitary chiral conformal field theory:
the formalism of unitary vertex operator algebras
[
DL14
,
CKLW18
]

(see
[
Kac98
,
FBZ04
,
LL04
]
for standard textbooks on VOAs),
and the formalism of chiral conformal nets
[
BMT88
,
BSM90
,
FRS92
,
GF93
,
BGL93
,
Was98
]
, building on the foundational work of Haag and Kastler
[
HK64
]
.
1
1

1

In this paper, we use the words chiral conformal net and conformal net interchangeably.

From a physical point of view, a chiral conformal field theory is not a stand-alone quantum field theory. It is instead a boundary theory for a 3-dimensional bulk TQFT.
2
2

2

This is in fact not a 3d TQFT in the sense usually understood in mathematics, as it suffers from a ‘gravitational anomaly’: it is a metric dependent theory, but a change of the space-time metric only affects the value of the correlators by a universal scalar
[
Wit89
, §2]
.

We quickly explain, with a physicist audience in mind, the notions of vertex operator algebras and of chiral conformal net:

•

Vertex o
p​​
erator al
g​
ebras
:
The VOA distils and axiomatizes the properties of the vector space of
boundary local operators
, along with their
operator product expansion
. It consists of a vector space
V
equipped with a ‘state-operator correspondence’

Y(-,z):V\,\,\to\,\,\End(V)[[z,z^{-1}]]

and a specified vector
\nu\in V
that is the stress-energy tensor (see Definition (
3.1
)).
A
unitary
VOA comes additionally equipped with an anti-linear involution
\Theta:V\to V
that implements the coordinate change
z\mapsto\bar{z}
.
For
a,b,c\in V
, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the inner product
\langle\Theta(c),Y(a,z)b\rangle
describes the correlator
\langle a(z)b(0)c(\infty)\rangle
on
{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}
, with the three local operators
a,b,c
inserted as the points
z,0,\infty
, respectively, and the whole thing placed on the boundary of a
3
-ball.

•

Conformal nets
:
Chiral conformal nets axiomatize the
Hilbert space associated to a Cauchy slice
of a cylindrical spacetime
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. The Hilbert space is called the ‘vacuum sector’ of the conformal net, and denoted
H_{0}
. Following Haag-Kastler
[
HK64
]
, for every open subset
{\mathcal{O}}\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
of the disc, one then has an associated
von Neumann algebra of observables
localised in
{\mathcal{O}}
.
If
{\mathcal{O}}\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
is a regular neighbourhood of an interval
I={\mathcal{O}}\cap\partial{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, then the bulk geometry of
{\mathcal{O}}
does not affect the algebra of observables: we get a map

{\mathcal{A}}:\{\text{intervals in $S^{1}$}\}\to\{\text{von Neumann algebras on $H_{0}$}\},

which is the data of the conformal net (see Definition
2.2
for more details).
Conformal symmetry is encoded by an action of
\Diff(S^{1})
on
H_{0}
.

In the landmark paper
[
CKLW18
]
,
it was shown that the formalism of
unitary vertex operator algebras
and the formalism of chiral conformal nets
are strongly interconnected in the sense that every unitary vertex operator algebra
V
satisfying a certain technical assumption called
strong locality
yields a chiral conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}_{V}
, and that it is possible to recover
V
from
{\mathcal{A}}_{V}
.

The main result of our paper is that every chiral conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
has an associated unitary vertex operator algebra
V_{\mathcal{A}}
.
(Throughout this paper, we assume that the generator of rotations
L_{0}
acts on the vaccum Hilbert space
H_{0}
with finite-dimensional eigenspaces.)

Theorem A
.

For every conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
with vacuum Hilbert space
H_{0}
, there is a natural structure of a unitary vertex operator algebra on the space of finite-energy vectors
V\subset H_{0}
.

This resolves a slightly weakened version of
[
CKLW18
, Conj. 9.4]
.
The vertex algebra structure on
V
is established in Corollary
7.4
, conformal symmetry of the vertex algebra is Theorem
7.11
, and unitarity is Theorem
7.13
.

In future work that will appear very shortly, we will
introduce a slight simplification of the notion of strong locality from
[
CKLW18
]
, which we call
AQFT-locality
, that avoids a certain technical requirement (polynomial energy bounds), but is still sufficient for generating a chiral conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}_{V}
.
We will prove that the vertex algebra
V_{\mathcal{A}}
associated to a conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
is AQFT-local, and that
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{V_{\mathcal{A}}}
.
As in
[
CKLW18
, Conj. 8.18]
, we conjecture that every unitary vertex operator algebra is AQFT-local, and that the inclusion
\{
conformal nets
\}\subset\{
unitary VOAs
\}
that we get is in fact an equality:

The situation as understood
before our work
by our work
conjecturally
uVOAs
CNets
uVOAs
CNets
uVOAs
=
CNets

We now explain our construction of a vertex algebra from a conformal net.
Fix a conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, with vacuum Hilbert space
H_{0}
.
The construction of the vertex algebra structure on the space
V\subset H_{0}
of finite energy vectors proceeds in two main steps.
Given a family of disjoint intervals
I_{j}
embedded
inside
the unit disc, and bounded operators
x_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})
3
3

3

We use here the ‘coordinate-free’ description of conformal nets, which assigns von Neumann algebras to arbitrary intervals, not just subintervals of the unit circle
[
BDH15
]
. Alternatively, one can parametrize the intervals
I_{j}
by intervals of
S^{1}
; this parametrization should then be considered as part of the data of
x_{j}
.
, we construct in Section
5
a vector
|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle\in H_{0}

(1)

Informally, we call these ‘worm-shaped insertions’ in the unit disc, in contrast to the point insertions considered below.
If each of the intervals
I_{j}
lies on the boundary of the disc we have
|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle=x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\Omega
.
In general, the vectors
|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle
are determined by that property in combination with a certain compatibility condition with the action of the
semigroup of annuli
, which we now describe.

An
annulus
is represented by a pair of Jordan curves
C^{\infty}(S^{1})\to{\mathbb{C}}
, with one curve encircling the other. The collection of all annuli, denoted
\Ann
, forms a semigroup under the operation of conformal welding.
As in
[
HT24
]
, we allow the Jordan curves to touch but not cross, so that the annulus might be ‘thin’ along part of the boundary. The following is an example of a thin annulus:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\mapsto\,\,\,\,\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{4}]:H_{0}\to H_{0}.

Moreover, if
\underline{A}
is supported in an interval
I\subset S^{1}
(the boundary parametrizations agree outside of
I

5
5

5

The property of
\underline{A}
being supported
I
is in fact slightly stronger than simply requiring that the two boundary parametrizations to agree outside of
I
(see Definition
4.12
).
), then
\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
.

The next step of our construction, in Section
6
, assigns vectors (resp. operators) to discs (resp. annuli) with
point
insertions
6
6

6

These are called
local operators
in the physics terminology.
.
More precisely, given finite-energy vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V\subset H_{0}
and distinct points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
lying in the interior of the unit disc, we construct vectors
|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle\in H_{0}
.
Similarly, if
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
and the points
z_{j}
lie in the interior of the annulus, then we define a bounded operator
\underline{A}[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]\in B(H_{0})
, which lies in
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
when
\underline{A}
is localized in
I
.
A key insight of this paper is that point insertions can be realized as finite linear combinations of worm-shaped insertions:

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.72287pt}{10.50235pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The vectors
|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle
depend holomorphically on the
z_{i}
, and satisfy a geometric analog of the vertex algebra axioms, which yields a vertex algebra structure on
V
; this is described in Section
7.2
.
We then show in Section
7.3
that there is a natural conformal vector for
V
, and that the associated representation of Virasoro algebra exponentiates to the diffeomorphism symmetry of the original net
{\mathcal{A}}
. Finally, in Section
7.4
, we show that the resulting VOA satisfies all the axioms of a
unitary
vertex operator algebra.

The article is organised as follows.
In Section
2
we give some background information on conformal nets, including the coordinate-free version of conformal nets introduced in
[
BDH15
]
, and in Section
3
we provide some background on vertex operator algebras.
Section
4
introduces the semigroup of annuli, along with its central extensions and representation theory
[
HT24
,
HT25
]
.
In Section
5
, we construct from an arbitrary conformal net vectors (and operators) associated to worm-shaped insertions in discs (and annuli).
In Section
6
, we construct the analogous vectors and operators associated to point insertions in discs and annuli.
Finally, in Section
7
, we construct a unitary VOA using the point insertions considered in the previous section.

2
Conformal nets

Let
{\mathbb{D}}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|\leq 1\}
be the
standard unit disc
, and

S^{1}=\partial{\mathbb{D}}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|=1\}
the
standard circle
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
disc
is a complex manifold with boundary which is holomorphically equivalent to
{\mathbb{D}}
.
A
circle
is a smooth oriented
1
-manifold diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
.
An
interval
is a smooth oriented
1
-manifold with boundary diffeomorphic to
[0,1]
.

Let
\Diff(S^{1})
be the group of orientation preserving diffeomorphisms of
S^{1}
, and
\Mob:=\Aut({\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathit{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
its subgroup of Möbius transformations.

Definition 2.2
.

A
conformal net

{\mathcal{A}}
consists of:

•

A Hilbert space
H_{0}
called the
vacuum sector
.

•

A unit vector
\Omega\in H_{0}
called the
vacuum vector
.

•

A strongly continuous representation
\Mob\to U(H_{0})
with one-dimensional fix-point space spanned by
\Omega
.
The infinitesimal generator of rotations
L_{0}
is required to have positive spectrum, and finite dimensional eigenspaces
7
7

7

It is plausible that the finite-dimensionality of the
L_{0}
-eigenspaces follows from the other axioms of conformal nets (even though, in the absence of
\Diff(S^{1})
-covariance, this is known to be false).
.

•

A strongly continuous projective representation
\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H_{0})
extending the action of
\Mob
.

•

An assignment

(I\subset S^{1})\,\,\,\mapsto\,\,\,({\mathcal{A}}(I)\subset B(H_{0}))

of a von Neumann algebra to every interval
I\subset S^{1}
.
This assignment should be
\Diff(S^{1})
-equivariant,
and satisfy

(I\subset J)\,\,\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,({\mathcal{A}}(I)\subset{\mathcal{A}}(J)),\vskip-5.69054pt

and
(\mathring{I}\cap\mathring{J}=\emptyset)\,\Rightarrow\,([{\mathcal{A}}(I),{\mathcal{A}}(J)]=0)
.

Moreover, if
\varphi\in\Diff(S^{1})
fixes
I
pointwise, then
\varphi
should also fix
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
pointwise.

Finally, the vacuum vector should be cyclic for the joint actions of the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
.

As a consequence of the above axioms we have the Reeh-Schlieder theorem, which states that
\Omega
is cyclic and separating for each algebra
{\mathcal{A}}(I)

[
GF93
, Cor. 2.8]
(see also
[
RS61
,
Bor65
]
).
We also have the celebrated Haag duality theorem
[
GF93
, Thm. 2.19(ii)]
(see also
[
BSM90
]
,
[
Lon08
, Thm. 6.2.3]
):

{\mathcal{A}}(I^{\prime})={\mathcal{A}}(I)^{\prime},

where
A^{\prime}:=\{b\in B(H_{0}):ab=ba\,\,\forall a\in A\}
denotes the commutant of a von Neumann algebra
A\subset B(H_{0})
, and
I^{\prime}:=S^{1}\setminus\mathring{I}
denotes the closed complement of an interval
I\subset S^{1}
.

We write
H_{0}^{f.e.}
for the algebraic direct sum of the
L_{0}
-eigenspaces of
H_{0}
, and call it the space of
finite energy vectors
. It is a dense subspace of
H_{0}
of countable dimension.

Definition 2.3
.

A
representation
of a conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
is a Hilbert space
K
equipped with actions
\rho_{I}:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to B(K)
for every interval
I\subset S^{1}
, which are
compatible in the sense that
\rho_{J}|_{{\mathcal{A}}(I)}=\rho_{I}
whenever
I\subset J
.

A representation is called
irreducible
if it cannot be written as a direct sum of other representations.

Let
\Diff_{I}(S^{1})\subset\Diff(S^{1})
be the group of diffeomorphisms supported in
I\subset S^{1}
.
By the axioms of conformal nets, the operator
U_{\varphi}\in PU(H_{0})
associated to a diffeomorphism

\varphi\in\Diff_{I}(S^{1})
commutes with
{\mathcal{A}}(I^{\prime})
;
by Haag duality, it is thus in
PU({\mathcal{A}}(I))
.
For every interval
I\subset S^{1}
, the action
\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H_{0})
therefore restricts to a homomorphism

\Diff_{I}(S^{1})\to PU({\mathcal{A}}(I)).

Given a representation
K
of the conformal net, we thus get a compatible collection of maps

\Diff_{I}(S^{1})\to PU(K)

(2)

hence a homomorphism

\mathrm{colim}_{I\subset S^{1}}\Diff_{I}(S^{1})\to PU(K).

The above colimit was computed in
[
Hen19
, Thm. 11]
,
and shown to be canonically isomorphic to the universal cover
\tDiff(S^{1})
of
\Diff(S^{1})
.
So we get a homomorphism

\tDiff(S^{1})\to PU(K)

(3)

for every representation
K
of our conformal net.

Let
\tRot(S^{1})\subset\tDiff(S^{1})
be the universal cover of the subgroup of rigid rotations, and let
L_{0}
be
the infinitesimal generator of the action
\tRot(S^{1})\to PU(K)
.
This is a self-adjoint unbounded operator on
K
, well defined up to an additive constant (one may fix the constant by considering the action of the universal cover of
\Mob
, but we shall not need this here
8
8

8

In
[
Wei06
]
, it is proven that this operator is always positive.

).
In this paper, we shall always assume that our conformal net representations have the property that
L_{0}
has discrete spectrum.
(A general representation can be written as direct integral of irreducible ones
[
KLM01
, App. C]
, and every irreducible representation has the property that
L_{0}
has discrete spectrum.)

Definition 2.4
.

Given a representation
K
of a conformal net,
we write
K^{f.e.}
for the algebraic direct sum of the
L_{0}
-eigenspaces of
K
, and call it the space of
finite energy vectors
of
K
.

Given a conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, following
[
BDH15
]
, one can form a corresponding “coordinate-free conformal net”, still denoted
{\mathcal{A}}
. The latter is a functor from the category of intervals (no longer subsets of
S^{1}
) and embeddings to the category of von Neumann algebras (no longer subalgebras of
B(H_{0})
) and injective
*
-algebra homomorphisms.
Orientation preserving embeddings go to
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear homomorphisms, and orientation reversing embeddings go to
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear anti-homomorphisms.

Furthermore, for every circle
S
, there is the notion of an
S
-sector.
This is a Hilbert space
K
equipped with compatible actions
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to B(K)
of all the von Neumann algebras associated to intervals
I\subset S

(for
S^{1}
the standard circle, this recovers the notion of a representation of
{\mathcal{A}}
).
Finally, every diffeomorphism
\varphi:S_{1}\to S_{2}
induces a pullback functor
\varphi^{*}
from the category of
S_{2}
-sectors to the category of
S_{1}
-sectors.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
K_{1}
be an
S_{1}
-sector,
K_{2}
an
S_{2}
-sector, and
\varphi:S_{1}\to S_{2}
a diffeomorphism.
A unitary
U:K_{1}\to K_{2}
is said to
implement

\varphi
if it is a morphism of
S_{1}
-sectors from
K_{1}
to
\varphi^{*}(K_{2})
.
Equivalently,
U
implements
\varphi
if it satisfies

UxU^{*}=\varphi(x)

for every
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
and
I\subset S_{1}
.

Remark 2.6
.

The homomorphism
U:\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H_{0})
in the definition of conformal net sends a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff(S^{1})
to the unique-up-to-phase
unitary
U(\varphi):H_{0}\to H_{0}
that implements it.

If
D
is a disc with boundary
S=\partial D
, then we can associate to it a canonical
S
-sector

H_{0}(D):=\Conf({\mathbb{D}},D)\times_{\Mob}H_{0}

(4)

called the
vacuum sector of
{\mathcal{A}}
associated to
D

(see
[
BDH15
, Thm. 2.13]
for a version of this construction).
It admits actions
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to B(H_{0}(D))
,
for every
I\subset\partial D
,
given by

x[(\varphi,\xi)]:=[(\varphi,{\varphi}^{-1}(x)\xi)],

(5)

where
{\varphi}^{-1}(x)
denotes the image of
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
under the map
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(\varphi^{-1}(I))
induced by
\varphi^{-1}
,
and
[(\varphi,\xi)]\in H_{0}(D)
denotes the equivalence class of
(\varphi,\xi)\in\Conf({\mathbb{D}},D)\times H_{0}
.
The vacuum sector
H_{0}(D)
also admits a canonical vector

\Omega_{D}\in H_{0}(D)
,
called the
vacuum vector associated to
D
,
defined as the equivalence class of
(\varphi,\Omega)
for any
\varphi\in\Conf({\mathbb{D}},D)
.

Finally, when
D
is the standard disc, the vacuum sector
H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
is canonically identified with
H_{0}
,
and the vacuum vector
\Omega_{\mathbb{D}}\in H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
maps to
\Omega\in H_{0}
under that identification.

3
Vertex operator algebras

Definition 3.1
.

An
\mathbb{N}
-graded
vertex operator algebra
(VOA) consists of a vector space
V=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V(n)
with
\dim(V(0))=1
,
\dim(V(n))<\infty
, two distinguished vectors
\Omega\in V(0)
and
\nu\in V(2)
, and a
state-field correspondence

v\,\,\mapsto\,\,Y(v,z)\in\End(V)[[z,z^{-1}]]

satisfying:

•

For any vector
v\in V
, we have

Y(\Omega,z)v=v
and
Y(v,z)\Omega=v+\mathcal{O}(z)
.

•

(
locality axiom
)
\forall u,v\in V
, the commutator
[Y(u,z),Y(v,w)]
is killed by
(z-w)^{N}
for
N>\!\!>0
.

•

The operators
L_{n}
defined by

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}L_{n}z^{-n-2}=Y(\nu,z)
satisfy the Virasoro algebra relations:
[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n}+\tfrac{c}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0}
for some scalar
c
.

•

L_{0}
is the grading operator

•

Y(L_{-1}v,z)=\tfrac{d}{dz}Y(v,z)
.

The number
c
is called the
central charge
of the VOA.

Remark 3.2
.

Writing
Y(v,z)=\sum v_{(n)}z^{-n-1}
, the locality axiom is known to be equivalent to the Borcherds identities (see e.g.
[
Kac98
, §4.8]
):

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{m}{j}\big(u_{(n+j)}v\big)_{(m+k-j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j}u_{(m+n-j)}v_{(k+j)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j+n}\binom{n}{j}v_{(n+k-j)}u_{(m+j)}.

Given a vertex algebra
V=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V(n)
, we define
\widehat{V}:=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}V(n)
to be the product of its weight spaces.
For any vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
and complex numbers
|z_{1}|>\ldots>|z_{n}|\geq 0
, the expression

Y(v_{1},z_{1})\ldots Y(v_{n},z_{n})\Omega

converges in
\widehat{V}
.
This is a holomorphic function of the
z_{i}
in the domain
|z_{1}|>\ldots>|z_{n}|\geq 0
that extends (uniquely) to a holomorphic function on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus\Delta:=\{(z_{i})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}:z_{i}\neq z_{j}\}
.
Moreover, for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, the functions defined by the above procedure starting from

Y(v_{1},z_{1})\ldots Y(v_{n},z_{n})\Omega

and from

Y(v_{\sigma(1)},z_{\sigma(1)})\ldots Y(v_{\sigma(n)},z_{\sigma(n)})\Omega

agree as
\widehat{V}
-valued functions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus\Delta
.

Definition 3.3
.

A
unitary vertex operator algebra
is an
\mathbb{N}
-graded vertex operator algebra equipped with an inner product
\langle\,,\,\rangle:V\times V\to{\mathbb{C}}
and an involution
\Theta:V\to V
, called the
PCT involution
.
The involution
\Theta
is an anti-linear VOA automorphism, and the inner product
9
9

9

Here, either
z
is a formal variable, or
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is a number.
If
z
is treated as a number, then the inner product in the LHS is a map
V\times\widehat{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, while the one in the RHS is a map
\widehat{V}\times V\to{\mathbb{C}}
.
satisfies

\big\langle v,Y(u,z)v^{\prime}\big\rangle=\big\langle Y(u,z)^{\dagger}v,v^{\prime}\big\rangle

for all
u,v,v^{\prime}\in V
, where

\displaystyle Y(u,z)^{\dagger}\;\!:=

\displaystyle\;Y\big(\Theta\;\!e^{zL_{1}}(-z^{-2})^{L_{0}}u,\bar{z}^{-1}\big)

(6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;Y(e^{\bar{z}L_{1}}(-\bar{z}^{-2})^{L_{0}}\Theta u,\bar{z}^{-1})

(see Remark
3.5
for an explanation of this formula).
It is customary to also include the normalisation condition
\|\Omega\|=1
in the definition of unitary vertex operator algebra.

Let
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
be the group of formal power series
a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\ldots
with
a_{1}\neq 0
, under the operation of composition,
and let
\Aut_{0}({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\subset\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
be the subgroup where the coefficient of
z
is equal to
1
.
For any vertex operator algebra
V
, the subalgebra
\Vir_{>0}:=\mathrm{Span}\{L_{n}\}_{n>0}
of the Virasoro algebra acts locally nilpotently on
V
, and thus exponentiates to an action of
\Aut_{0}({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
.
Furthermore, since
V
has integer grading, the operator
L_{0}
exponentiates to an action of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. These assemble to an action
10
10

10

This is not an action by VOA automorphisms.

\Aut_{0}({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}=\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\to\End(V).

(7)

When
V
is a unitary vertex operator algebra, the above action extends to a bigger group that also contains the orientation reversing changes of coordinates:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
V
be a unitary VOA. Then the Virasoro operators
L_{n}
for
n\geq 0
and the anti-linear PCT operator
\Theta:V\to V
together induce an action of

\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2

on
V
(where the generator of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
is the map
t\mapsto\bar{t}
).

Proof.

As explained above,
\Vir_{\geq 0}
exponentiates to an action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
on
V
.
The PCT operator commutes with the Virasoro operators
L_{n}
because it is an automorphism of the VOA.
It satisfies
\Theta(\lambda L_{n})\Theta=\bar{\lambda}L_{n}
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and therefore
\Theta g\Theta=\bar{g}
for any
g\in\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
.
The latter is equivalent an action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
on
V
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The mysterious
\Theta e^{zL_{1}}(-z^{-2})^{L_{0}}u
which appears in (
6
) is just the action on
u

of the coordinate transformation
t\mapsto\overline{(t+z)}^{-1}-\bar{z}^{-1}

(the germ of
\zeta\mapsto\bar{\zeta}^{-1}
around
\zeta=z
). To see this, note that

\big(t\mapsto\overline{(t+z)}^{-1}-\bar{z}^{-1}\big)=h\circ f\circ g

where
h(t)=\bar{t}
,
f(t)=(t^{-1}-z)^{-1}
, and
g(t)=-z^{-2}t
.
The Mobius transformations
f
and
g
correspond to the operators
e^{zL_{1}}
and
(-z^{-2})^{L_{0}}
, respectively.

Modules for vertex operator algebras

Let
V
be a vertex operator algebra, and let
M=\bigoplus_{n}M(n)
be a vector space graded by (some subset of)
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, or, more generally, graded by some subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
whose real part is bounded below.

Definition 3.6
.

We say that
M
is a
module
for
V
if it comes equipped with a state-field correspondence

v\,\,\mapsto\,\,Y^{M}(v,z)\in\End(M)[[z,z^{-1}]]

which is degree preserving when the formal variable
z
is given degree
-1
, and satisfies:

•

Y^{M}(\Omega,z)=\id_{M}

•

For any
u,v\in V
, the commutator
[Y^{M}(u,z),Y^{M}(v,w)]
is killed by
(z-w)^{N}
for
N>\!\!>0
.

•

For any
u,v\in V
, the difference between
Y^{M}(Y(u,z)v,w)
and
Y^{M}(u,z+w)Y^{M}(v,w)
is killed by
(z+w)^{N}
for
N>\!\!>0
.

Writing
Y^{M}(v,z)=\sum v^{M}_{(n)}z^{-n-1}
,
the last two axioms are known to be equivalent to the Borcherds identities (see
[
LL04
, Prop. 4.4.1]
, in which the Borcherds identities are written in the notation of formal delta functions):

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{m}{j}\big(u_{(n+j)}v\big)^{M}_{(m+k-j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j}u_{(m+n-j)}^{M}v_{(k+j)}^{M}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j+n}\binom{n}{j}v_{(n+k-j)}^{M}u_{(m+j)}^{M}.

A
weak module
is a vector space
M
as above, but without any grading, equipped with a state-field correspondence subject to the lower truncation condition

Y^{M}(v,z)a\in M[[z]][z^{-1}]

\forall a\in M
, and subject to all the axioms above, except for the one requiring that the state-field correspondence is degree preserving.

Definition 3.7
.

A unitary module for a unitary VOA
V
is a
V
-module
M
equipped with an inner product
\langle\,\,,\,\rangle_{M}:M\times M\to{\mathbb{C}}
that satisfies

\big\langle a,Y(u,z)b\big\rangle_{M}=\big\langle Y(u,z)^{\dagger}a,b\big\rangle_{M}.

for all
u\in V
and
a,b\in M
.

4
The semigroup of annuli and its representations

4.1
The semigroup of annuli

The Lie algebra of
\Diff(S^{1})
is the set
{\mathcal{X}}:={\mathcal{X}}(S^{1})
of vector fields on
S^{1}
, equipped with the Lie bracket

\big[f(\theta)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta},g(\theta)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\big]:=(gf^{\prime}-fg^{\prime})\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}

(8)

(the opposite of the usual Lie bracket of vector fields).
Its complexification
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathcal{X}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
is known to have no associated Lie group.
But it has an associated semigroup, the
semigroup of annuli

\Ann

[
Seg87
,
Ner90
,
HT24
]
, which we now describe.

An
embedded annulus
is a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
of the form

A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}\subset{\mathbb{C}},

where
D_{in}\subset D_{out}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
are discs (closed subsets bounded by smooth Jordan curves).
Its inner and outer boundaries
\partial_{in}A:=\partial D_{in}
and
\partial_{out}A:=\partial D_{out}
are equipped with the orientations inherited from
D_{in/out}
.
Note that
\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A
is allowed to be non-empty, and that
\mathring{A}
is allowed to be disconnected, or even empty.
We equip
A
with the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
of functions that are continuous on
A
, holomorphic on
\mathring{A}
, and smooth on
\partial_{in}A
and on
\partial_{out}A
.

Definition 4.1
.

An
annulus
is a locally ringed space
A=(A,{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
which is isomorphic to an embedded annulus.
We also record the orientations of
\partial_{in}A
and
\partial_{out}A
as part of the data.
11
11

11

When
\mathring{A}\not=\emptyset
, the orientations of
\partial_{in}A
and
\partial_{out}A
can be deduced from the orientation of
\mathring{A}
.

An annulus
A
is called
thick
if
\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A=\emptyset
(in which case it is diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times[0,1]
).
It is called
thin
if
\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A
contains an interval, and

completely thin
if
\partial_{in}A=\partial_{out}A
(in which case it is diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Every annulus is isomorphic to an embedded annulus
A\subset{\mathbb{C}}
that satisfies
\partial_{out}A=S^{1}
and
0\not\in A
.

Proof.

By definition, every annulus is isomorphic to one of the form
D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.
Now apply the Riemann mapping theorem for domains with smooth boundary
[
Bel92
, Thm. 8.2]

to
D_{out}
.
∎

An
annulus with parametrised boundary
is an annulus equipped with an orientation reversing diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{in}:S^{1}\to\partial_{in}A
, and an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
\varphi_{out}:S^{1}\to\partial_{out}A
.

Definition 4.3
.

The
semigroup of annuli

\Ann
is the set of isomorphisms classes of annuli with parametrised boundary.
The semigroup operation is
conformal welding
, which
is the gluing of annuli along their boundaries:

\begin{split}\cup:\Ann&\times\Ann\,\longrightarrow\,\Ann\\
A_{1}&\cup A_{2}\,:=\,\mathrm{pushout}\big(A_{1}\xleftarrow{\,\varphi_{in}^{A_{1}}\,}S_{1}\xrightarrow{\,\varphi_{out}^{A_{2}}\,}A_{2}\big)\\
&\phantom{\cup A_{2}\,\,:}=\,A_{1}\sqcup A_{2}\big/\varphi_{in}^{A_{1}}(x)\sim\varphi_{out}^{A_{2}}(x).\end{split}

The annulus
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is equipped with the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}
specified by

f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}(U)\Leftrightarrow f|_{U\cap A_{i}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{i}}(U\cap A_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
We refer the reader to
[
HT24
, Prop. 3.5]
for a proof that
(A_{1}\cup A_{2},{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}})
is again an element of
\Ann
.

The semigroup of annuli is equipped with an involution
\dagger:\Ann\to\Ann

which sends an annulus
A
to the same annulus equipped with the opposite complex structure:
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\dagger}}(U)\Leftrightarrow\bar{f}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A}(U)
. The incoming and outgoing circles are exchanged, but their parametrisations remain the same.
This involution satisfies

(A\cup B)^{\dagger}=B^{\dagger}\cup A^{\dagger}.

(9)

Example 4.4
.

For
r\in(0,1]
, the round annulus
A_{r}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:r\leq|z|\leq 1\}
, with boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in}(z)=rz
and
\varphi_{out}(z)=z
is denoted
r^{\ell_{0}}\in\Ann
.

There is an obvious embedding

\Diff(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\Ann

which sends a diffeomorphism
\varphi
to the completely thin annulus
A_{\varphi}:=(S^{1},\varphi_{in}{=}\varphi,\varphi_{out}{=}\id)
.
The semigroup of annuli is, in some sense, a complexification of this subgroup.

Lemma 4.5
.

For every annulus
A\in\Ann
there exist
0<r\leq 1
,

B\in\Ann
, and
\varphi\in\Diff(S^{1})
such that

A_{\varphi}\cup A\cup B=r^{\ell_{0}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.2
, we may assume that
A={\mathbb{D}}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, with
0\not\in A
.
Let
\varphi^{A}_{in}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in}
and
\varphi^{A}_{out}\in\Diff(S^{1})
be the boundary parametrizations of
A
.
Pick
r
such that
D_{r}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|\leq r\}\subset D_{in}
, and let
B:=D_{in}\setminus\mathring{D}_{r}
with boundary parametrizations

\varphi^{B}_{in}(z)=rz
and
\varphi^{B}_{out}=\varphi^{A}_{in}
.
Then setting
\varphi=(\varphi^{A}_{out})^{-1}
, we have
A_{\varphi}\cup A\cup B=r^{\ell_{0}}
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

An annulus with parametrised boundary is said to be
localized in some interval

I\subset S^{1}
if its incoming and outgoing boundary parametrizations agree on the closed complement
I^{\prime}:=S^{1}\setminus\mathring{I}
of
I
:

\varphi_{in}|_{I^{\prime}}=\varphi_{out}|_{I^{\prime}}:I^{\prime}\to A.

(Such annuli are never thick.)
We denote by
\Ann(I)\subset\Ann
the sub-semigroup of annuli localized in
I
.

The semigroup of annuli contains another important sub-semigroup
\Univ\subset\Ann
, which behaves in many respects like the Borel subgroup of an algebraic group:

Definition 4.7
.

The
semigroup of univalent maps

\Univ
is the set of holomorphic embeddings
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
(with
f^{\prime}
is everywhere non-zero, including on
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
).
The inclusion
\Univ\hookrightarrow\Ann
sends a univalent map
f\in\Univ
to the annulus

A_{f}:={\mathbb{D}}\setminus f(\mathring{\mathbb{D}}),

with
\varphi_{in}=f
and
\varphi_{out}=\id
.

Within
\Univ
, we have a further sub-semigroup
\Univ_{0}
, consisting of the univalent maps which fix the point
0\in{\mathbb{D}}
.

Definition 4.8
.

A
disc with parametrised boundary
is a disc
D
(Definition
2.1
) equipped with an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
S^{1}\to\partial D
.

The (
C^{\infty}
version of the)
universal Teichmüller space

{\mathcal{T}}
is the set of isomorphism classes of disc with parametrised boundary.

The semigroup of annuli acts on the universal Teichmüller space by conformal welding, and the map

\Ann\to{\mathcal{T}}:A\mapsto A\cup{\mathbb{D}}

(10)

identifies
{\mathcal{T}}
with the quotient
\Ann/\Univ
(the quotient of
\Ann
by the equivalence relation generated by
A\cup B\sim A
, for all
B\in\Univ
).
The universal Teichmüller space is thus an analog of the flag manifold
G/B
of an algebraic group
G
.

4.2
Central extensions of the semigroup of annuli

For
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
, let

0\to{\mathbb{C}}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}\to 0

(11)

be the Lie algebra central extension determined by the Virasoro cocycle

\omega_{\Vir}(f,g)=\omega_{\Vir}\big(f(\theta)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta},g(\theta)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\big):=\tfrac{c}{12}\int_{S^{1}}(f^{\prime\prime\prime}(\theta)+f^{\prime}(\theta))\,g(\theta)\,\tfrac{d\theta}{2\pi i}.

(12)

Let
\ell_{n}:=-ie^{in\theta}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
12
12

12

In terms of the coordinate
z=e^{i\theta}
on
S^{1}
, this is
\ell_{n}=z^{n+1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}
,
and the Virasoro cocycle is given by
\omega_{\Vir}\big(f(z)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z},g(z)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\big)=\tfrac{c}{12}\int_{S^{1}}\tfrac{\partial^{3}f}{\partial z^{3}}(z)\,g(z)\,\tfrac{dz}{2\pi i}
.

and let
L_{n}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}
be its standard lift so that, using (
8
), we have the familiar

\big[L_{m},L_{n}\big]\,=\,(m-n)L_{m+n}+\tfrac{c}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0}
.
In particular, the Virasoro Lie algebra
\Vir_{c}:=\mathrm{Span}\{L_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}
is a dense subalgebra in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}
.

Associated to the central extension (
11
), there exists a central extension of semigroups

0\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}{\times}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\to\tAnn_{c}\to\Ann\to 0

(13)

that we describe shortly.
We first list some related central extensions that follow from the one above.
First, if we mod out
\tAnn_{c}
by the central
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, we get the universal cover of
\Ann
:

0\to{\mathbb{Z}}\to\tAnn\to\Ann\to 0.

Let
\tMob
be the universal cover of the Möbius group.
Since
\tMob
is also the universal central extension of
\Mob
,
the map
\tMob\to\Mob\to\Diff(S^{1})\to\Ann
lifts uniquely to a map
\tMob\to\tAnn_{c}
,
yielding a canonical copy of
{\mathbb{Z}}
in the center of
\tAnn_{c}
.
(I.e., the center of
\tAnn_{c}
is
canonically
isomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}{\times}{\mathbb{Z}}
.)

Definition 4.9
.

We let
\Ann_{c}
be the quotient of
\tAnn_{c}
by the central subgroup
{\mathbb{Z}}
described above.
This is a central extension of the semigroup of annuli by
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
:

0\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to\Ann_{c}\to\Ann\to 0.

(14)

If
I\subset S^{1}
is an interval, we also let
\Ann_{c}(I)
be the pullback of the central extension
\Ann_{c}
to
\Ann(I)
:

0\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to\Ann_{c}(I)\to\Ann(I)\to 0.

We call the elements of
\Ann_{c}

lifted annuli
, and the elements of
\tAnn_{c}

z
-lifted annuli
.

Our construction of
\tAnn_{c}
relies of the notion of
framing
of an annulus:

Definition 4.10
.

Let
A
be an annulus with parametrised boundary.
A
framing
of
A
is a smooth surjective map

h:S^{1}\times[0,1]\to A

whose restriction to each
S^{1}\times\{t\}
is an embedding,
satisfies
h|_{S^{1}\times\{0\}}=\varphi_{in}
and
h|_{S^{1}\times\{1\}}=\varphi_{out}
,
and for which
-\frac{\partial h/\partial t}{\partial h/\partial\theta}
has everywhere non-negative imaginary part.

The
path
associated to a framing
h
is the map
X:[0,1]\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
given by:

X(t):=\Big({-}\tfrac{\partial h/\partial t}{\partial h/\partial\theta}\Big)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}.

By
[
HT24
, Cor. 4.17]
, every annulus with parametrised boundary admits a framing.
Given a framing
h
with associated path
\{X(t)\}_{t\in[0,1]}
then, following
[
HT24
, §4]
, we can recover
A
from the path by a certain geometric procedure, which we think of,
and formally denote by a time-ordered exponential:

A=\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(X(\tau)d\tau\big).

Given an
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
-valued path
X
as above,
let
\{\widetilde{X}(t)\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be its image under the standard splitting
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}
of (
11
).
In
[
HT24
, §5]
, we constructed
\tAnn_{c}
as a certain set of equivalence classes of formal expressions

\underline{A}=z\,{\cdot}\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau\big),

(15)

where
\widetilde{X}
is as above, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.

We now describe the equivalence relation on the set of formal expressions (
15
).
If
A\in\Ann
is an annulus with parametrised boundary, and if
\{\widetilde{X}_{0}(t)\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\widetilde{X}_{1}(t)\}_{t\in[0,1]}

come from two framings
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
of
A
, then we declare

z_{0}\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}_{0}(\tau)d\tau\big)\,\,\,\sim\,\,\,z_{1}\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}_{1}(\tau)d\tau\big)

(16)

in
\tAnn_{c}
if there exists an (equivalently for every) isotopy
h(\theta,t,s)
between
h_{0}(\theta,t)
and
h_{1}(\theta,t)
such that

z_{1}\;\!z_{0}^{-1}\,=\,\exp\int\omega_{\Vir}\Big(\tfrac{\partial h/\partial t}{\partial h/\partial\theta},\tfrac{\partial h/\partial s}{\partial h/\partial\theta}\Big)\,dtds.

(17)

Let
\ast:\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}
be the continuous conjugate-linear extension of the map
L_{n}\mapsto L_{-n}
.
The dagger structure on
\Ann
lifts to a dagger structure on
\tAnn_{c}
given by

\Big(z\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau\big)\Big)^{\dagger}\,:=\,\bar{z}\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}(1-\tau)^{*}d\tau\big)

and satisfying

(\underline{A}\cup\underline{B})^{\dagger}=\underline{B}^{\dagger}\cup\underline{A}^{\dagger}.

(18)

Example 4.11
.

The annulus
r^{\ell_{0}}\in\Ann
from Example
4.4
admits a canonical
z
-lift
r^{L_{0}}\in\tAnn_{c}

coming from the framing
h(\theta,t)=e^{i\theta}r^{1-t}
of
A_{r}
.
In that case,
X(t)=i|\log(r)|
is independent of
t
, and corresponds to the vector field
\log(r)\ell_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
This lifts to
\log(r)L_{0}\in\Vir_{c}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{c}
, and our notation
r^{L_{0}}
is justified:

\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau\big)=\Exp\big(\log(r)L_{0}\big)=r^{L_{0}}.

When
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
f
,
g
in (
12
) are real-valued,
the Virasoro cocycle takes its values in
i{\mathbb{R}}
.
The same construction (
15
,
16
) as above
restricted to the case of completely thin annuli, and with
z\in U(1)
as opposed to
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
,
produces a central extension

0\to U(1){\times}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\to\tDiff_{c}(S^{1})\to\Diff(S^{1})\to 0.

(19)

Modding out by the canonical central
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we then also get an analog of (
13
)

0\to U(1)\to\Diff_{c}(S^{1})\to\Diff(S^{1})\to 0.

(20)

The central extensions (
19
), (
20
), (
13
), (
14
) fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tDiff_{c}(S^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Diff_{c}(S^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tAnn_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ann_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where each vertical arrow is the inclusion of a real Lie group in its “complexification”.
For
\varphi\in\Diff_{c}(S^{1})
or
\tDiff_{c}(S^{1})
, we shall write
\underline{A}{}_{\varphi}
for the corresponding element of
\Ann_{c}
or
\tAnn_{c}
.

The above semigroups are further equipped with anti-linear involutions
that we now describe.
To start, the continuous
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear extensions of the maps

\lambda\ell_{n}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}\ell_{n}

and

\lambda L_{n}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}L_{n}
(where
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
) yield actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
on

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and

\widetilde{{\mathcal{X}}}_{c}
by Lie algebras automorphisms. Those integrate to actions

{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\,\hskip 2.84544pt{\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\qbezier{}{7}{6}{4.5}{8.3}{2}{7}\qbezier{}{2}{7}{-1.5}{4}{2}{1}\qbezier{}{2}{1}{4.5}{-0.3}{7}{2}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{6.1}{7.5}{6.8}{9}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{5}{6.1}{4.2}{4.4}\end{picture}\hskip 2.84544pt}\tAnn\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\,\hskip 2.84544pt{\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\qbezier{}{7}{6}{4.5}{8.3}{2}{7}\qbezier{}{2}{7}{-1.5}{4}{2}{1}\qbezier{}{2}{1}{4.5}{-0.3}{7}{2}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{6.1}{7.5}{6.8}{9}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{5}{6.1}{4.2}{4.4}\end{picture}\hskip 2.84544pt}\tAnn_{c}

that preserve
\tMob
, and hence the central
{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\tMob
(on which they act by
-1
).
They therefore descend to actions

{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\,\hskip 2.84544pt{\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\qbezier{}{7}{6}{4.5}{8.3}{2}{7}\qbezier{}{2}{7}{-1.5}{4}{2}{1}\qbezier{}{2}{1}{4.5}{-0.3}{7}{2}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{6.1}{7.5}{6.8}{9}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{5}{6.1}{4.2}{4.4}\end{picture}\hskip 2.84544pt}\Ann\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\,\hskip 2.84544pt{\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\qbezier{}{7}{6}{4.5}{8.3}{2}{7}\qbezier{}{2}{7}{-1.5}{4}{2}{1}\qbezier{}{2}{1}{4.5}{-0.3}{7}{2}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{6.1}{7.5}{6.8}{9}\qbezier{}{7}{6}{5}{6.1}{4.2}{4.4}\end{picture}\hskip 2.84544pt}\Ann_{c}.

(21)

Note that the involution (
21
) on
\Ann
admits the following more elementary description,
as the map
\Ann\to\Ann:A\mapsto\overline{A}
which sends an annulus
A\in\Ann
, with boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}
, to that same annulus with opposite complex structure, and boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}\circ(z\mapsto\bar{z})
.

Definition 4.12
.

If an annulus
A\in\Ann
is localised in
I\subset S^{1}
, then a
z
-lift
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is said to be
localised in
I
if it can be written as

\underline{A}=z\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau),

where each
\widetilde{X}(\tau)
covers a vector field
X(\tau)\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
supported in
I
.
We write
\tAnn_{c}(I)\subset\tAnn_{c}
for the sub-semigroup of
z
-lifted annuli that are localised in
I
.

The projection
\tAnn_{c}\to\Ann_{c}
maps
\tAnn_{c}(I)
isomorphically onto
\Ann_{c}(I)
.
We may thus drop the distinction between
\tAnn_{c}(I)
and
\Ann_{c}(I)
, and treat

\Ann_{c}(I)
as a sub-semigroup of both
\Ann_{c}
and
\tAnn_{c}
.

Let
\tUniv
denote the universal cover of
\Univ
, and let us recall that

{\mathcal{T}}=\Ann/\Univ=\tAnn/\tUniv
.
By
[
HT24
, Prop. 4.22]
, every element
A\in\tUniv
can be written as

A=\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp(X(\tau)d\tau),

(22)

with each
X(\tau)
in the
univalent subalgebra

\overline{\mathrm{Span}}\{\ell_{n}\}_{n\geq-1}
of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
(In terms of the coordinate
z=e^{i\theta}
, the univalent subalgebra consists of vector fields
f(z)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}
with
f
extending holomorphically to the unit disc.)
Let us define a
univalent framing
to be a framing
h
such that the corresponding path
X
takes values in the univalent subalgebra.
Any pair of univalent framings of
A\in\tUniv
are homotopic through univalent framings: they are homotopic through framings by
[
HT24
, Lem. 4.18]
, and these framings may be modified to be univalent as in the proof of
[
HT24
, Prop. 4.22]
.
A choice of univalent framing induces a lift
of
A
to
\tAnn_{c}
.
Moreover, since the Virasoro cocycle vanishes on the univalent subalgebra, this lift is independent of the choice of univalent framing (because the integral in the right hand side of (
17
) vanishes).
It follows that the central extension
\tAnn_{c}\to\tAnn
uniquely trivialises over
\tUniv
, and we may thus view
\tUniv
as a sub-semigroup of
\tAnn_{c}
.
Modding out by the central
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we also get an embedding
\Univ\hookrightarrow\Ann_{c}
.

Definition 4.13
.

The quotient space

{\mathcal{T}}_{c}\,:=\,\Ann_{c}/\Univ\,=\,\tAnn_{c}/\tUniv.

is a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-torsor over the universal Teichmüller space
{\mathcal{T}}
.
Its elements are called
lifted discs
.

Since the action of
\Ann_{c}
on
{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
covers the action of
\Ann
on
{\mathcal{T}}
by conformal welding, we shall denote it by the same symbol:
given
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
and
\underline{D}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
, we write
\underline{A}\cup\underline{D}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
for the action of
\underline{A}
on
\underline{D}
.
We shall also abuse notation and write
{\mathbb{D}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
for the standard lift of the the standard disc
{\mathbb{D}}\in{\mathcal{T}}
(defined as the image of
1\in\Ann_{c}
under the quotient map
\Ann_{c}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
).

Given a lifted disc
\underline{D}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
with underlying disc
D
, and given an embedding
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to D
, there is a unique lift
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
of
A:=D\setminus f({\mathbb{D}})
that satisfies
\underline{A}\cup{\mathbb{D}}=\underline{D}
.
We write
\underline{D}\setminus f({\mathbb{D}})
for this unique element of
\Ann_{c}
so that,
by definition, we have

(\underline{D}\setminus f({\mathbb{D}}))\cup{\mathbb{D}}\,=\,\underline{D}.

4.3
Representations of the semigroup of annuli

The conjugate-linear involution
*:\Vir_{c}\to\Vir_{c}
given on generators by
L_{n}\mapsto L_{-n}
equips the Virasoro algebra with the structure of a
*
-Lie algebra.

Definition 4.14
.

A
unitary representation
of the Virasoro algebra on a Hilbert space
H

is map
\pi
from
\Vir_{c}
to the set of closed unbounded operators on
H
satisfying:

•

\pi(1)=1

•

\pi(X^{*})=\pi(X)^{*}

•

\pi
restricts to a Lie algebra homomorphism
\Vir_{c}\to\End(H^{f.e.})
,
where
H^{f.e.}
denotes the algebraic direct sum of the eigenspaces of
\pi(L_{0})
.
13
13

13

If we wished to also accommodate representations in which
\pi(L_{0})
has continuous spectrum, then
H^{f.e.}
should be instead defined as the algebraic span of the ranges of the finite spectral projections of
\pi(L_{0})
.

A unitary representation
(H,\pi)
is said to have
positive energy
if
\pi(L_{0})
is a positive operator with discrete spectrum and finite dimensional eigenspaces.

We have shown in
[
HT25
]

that every positive energy unitary representation
\pi
of
\Vir_{c}
integrates to a holomorphic representation of
\tAnn_{c}
, denoted again
\pi
.
Given a
z
-lifted annulus
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
, represented as
\underline{A}=z\,{\cdot}\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau\big)
, we define in loc. cit.

\pi\Big(z\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp(\widetilde{X}(\tau)d\tau)\Big)\,:=\,z\,{\cdot}\!\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp\big(\pi(\widetilde{X}(\tau))d\tau\big).

(23)

Here, the time-ordered exponential in the right-hand side is, by definition, the value at
t=1
of the solution of the evolution equation

\tfrac{d}{dt}Y_{t}=\pi(\widetilde{X}(t))Y_{t}
(see
[
HT25
, §4]
for the proof of existence of uniqueness of solutions to that evolution equation).
The representation (
23
) is compatible with the involutions in the sense that

\pi(\underline{A}^{\dagger})=\pi(\underline{A})^{*}.

(24)

When
H=H_{0}
, this action of
\tAnn_{c}
on
H_{0}
descends to an action of
\Ann_{c}
.
Also, as an immediate consequence of the formula (
22
) for univalent annuli, along with the fact that the operators
L_{n}
for
n\geq-1

kill the vacuum vector
\Omega\in H_{0}
, we have

\pi(\underline{A})\Omega=\Omega

(25)

for every lifted annulus
\underline{A}
in the image of the embedding
\Univ\hookrightarrow\Ann_{c}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

The operator
\pi(\underline{A}):H\to H
associated to a
z
-lifted annulus
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is injective with dense image.

Proof.

An operator is injective if and only if its adjoint has dense image.
So, by (
24
), it is enough to just check injectivity.
Applying Lemma
4.5
to the conjugate annulus
\underline{A}^{\dagger}
,
we may write
r^{L_{0}}=\underline{B}\cup\underline{A}\cup\underline{A}{}_{\varphi}

for some
\underline{B}\in\tAnn_{c}
and
\varphi\in\tDiff_{c}(S^{1})
.
It follows that

\pi(r^{L_{0}})=r^{\pi(L_{0})}=\pi(\underline{B})\pi(\underline{A})\pi(\underline{A}{}_{\varphi}).

The left hand side is the exponential of a self-adjoint operator, hence injective.
The operator
\pi(\underline{A}{}_{\varphi})
is invertible.
It follows that
\pi(\underline{A})
is injective.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net, let
H_{0}
be its vacuum sector, and let
U_{0}:\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H_{0})
be the projective representation of
\Diff(S^{1})
on
H_{0}
.
Recall that all our conformal net representations are assumed to have discrete
L_{0}
spectrum.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
(H,\rho)
be a representation of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Then there exists a unique continuous linear map

f\mapsto T_{H}(f)

from
C^{\infty}(S^{1})
to the space of closed unbounded operators on
H
with core
H^{f.e.}
(with the topology of pointwise convergence on
H^{f.e.}
​) such that:

•

The operators
L_{n}:=T_{H}(e^{in\theta})
satisfy the Virasoro algebra relations on
H^{f.e.}
.

•

\rho_{I}(U_{0}(\exp(f\tfrac{d}{d\theta})))=\exp(iT_{H}(f))
up to phase, for any real-valued function
f
with support in
I
.

These operators satisfy
T_{H}(f)^{*}=T_{H}(\bar{f})
,
and
L_{0}:=T_{H}(1)
agrees (up to constant) with the operator used in Definition
2.4
.
Moreover, for any function
f
with support in
I
, the operator
T_{H_{0}}(f)
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
, and

\rho_{I}(T_{H_{0}}(f))=T_{H}(f).

(26)

The operator-valued distribution
f\mapsto T_{H}(f)
is called the
stress energy tensor
of
H
.

Proof.

We first prove uniqueness of the stress-energy tensor.
For
f
real-valued and supported in some interval
I
, the formula

\qquad\qquad\quad iT_{H}(f)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\Big(\exp(iT_{H}(tf))\Big)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\Big(\rho_{I}\big(U_{0}(\exp(tf\tfrac{d}{d\theta}))\big)\Big)\qquad\quad(\mathrm{mod}\,\,{\mathbb{C}}{\cdot}1_{H})

determines
T_{H}(f)
up to an additive constant in terms of the projective action of
\Diff(S^{1})
on
H_{0}
.
The linearity of
f\mapsto T_{H}(f)

(along with the fact that finite-energy vectors are assumed to be a core) then also determines
T_{H}(f)
up to a constant in the
absence of any reality and support conditions.
The requirement that
L_{n}
satisfy the Virasoro algebra relations fixes the constant when
f
is a trigonometric polynomial,
and the continuity assumption fixes the constant for all
f
.

We now prove existence.
Let
c
be the central charge of
{\mathcal{A}}
, and let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be the Virasoro sub-net, given by

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)=\big\{\exp(iT_{H_{0}}(f)):\supp(f)\subset I\text{ and }f\text{ is real-valued}\big\}^{\prime\prime}.

(27)

The characterising property

\rho_{I}\big(U_{0}(\exp(f\tfrac{d}{d\theta}))\big)=\exp(iT_{H}(f))

of
T_{H}(f)
is stated purely in terms of the elements
U_{0}(\exp(f\tfrac{d}{d\theta}))
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)
(and the definition of
H^{f.e.}\!
also only uses the action of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
),
so we may assume without loss of generality that
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
.
By decomposing
H
into irreducible
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
-reps (using our assumption that
L_{0}
has discrete spectrum), we may furthermore assume that
H
is irreducible.
Irreducible representations of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
have been classified in
[
Car04
, Prop. 2.1]
,
[
Wei05
, Cor. 6.4.7]
, and
[
Wei17
, Thm. 5.6]
: they are all isomorphic to some
H_{c,h}
, characterised by a choice of minimal energy
h\geq 0
, which we now describe.
Let
M=M_{c,h}
be the simple unitary module for the Virasoro algebra
\Vir_{c}
with minimal energy
h
, and let
H=H_{c,h}
be its Hilbert space completion, so that
H^{f.e.}=M
.
Then
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
acts on
H
in the following way:

For
f=\sum a_{n}e^{in\theta}\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
, let
T_{H}(f)
be the closure of
\sum a_{n}L_{n}
acting on
M
.
These operators satisfy
T_{H}(f)^{*}=T_{H}(\overline{f})
(see
[
Wei05
, §3.2.2]
).
Consider the action
U:\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H)
uniquely specified by the requirement that

U\big(\exp(f\tfrac{d}{d\theta}))\big)=\exp\big(iT_{H}(f)\big)

(28)

when
f
is real-valued
[
GW85
,
TL99
]
.
14
14

14

This defines a projective action of
\Diff(S^{1})
because
H
is irreducible. Otherwise, it would only give a projective action of its universal cover
\tDiff(S^{1})
.

In the same way, we have
T_{H_{0}}(f)
acting on the vacuum sector
H_{0}=H_{c,0}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}
, and
U_{0}:\Diff(S^{1})\to PU(H_{0})
given by
U_{0}(\exp(f\tfrac{d}{d\theta})))=\exp(iT_{H_{0}}(f))
.
By (
27
), the operator
T_{H_{0}}(f)
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)
when
\supp(f)\subset I
and
f
is real-valued.
Since
T_{H_{0}}(f+g)
is the closure of
T_{H_{0}}(f)+T_{H_{0}}(g)
(see
[
HT25
, Lem. 4.1]
), it follows that
T_{H_{0}}(f)
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)
even for complex-valued functions with
\supp(f)\subset I
.

The action of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)
on
H
is given by
[
Car04
, Eqn. (31)]

\rho_{I}\big(\exp(iT_{H_{0}}(f))\big)=\exp(iT_{H}(f))

(29)

for
f
real-valued. In particular,

i\rho_{I}(T_{H_{0}}(f))=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\Big(\rho_{I}(\exp(iT_{H_{0}}(tf)))\Big)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\Big(\exp(iT_{H}(tf))\Big)=iT_{H}(f).

By linearity (using that

T_{H}(f+ig)
is the closure of
T_{H}(f)+iT_{H}(g)

[
HT25
, Lem. 4.1]
), it follows that
\rho_{I}(T_{H_{0}}(f))=T_{H}(f)
holds for all functions
f
, not just real-valued ones.

To finish the proof, we must still show that
L_{0}:=T_{H}(1)
agrees (up to constant) with the operator used in Definition
2.4
.
By (
29
) and by continuity, the action of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Vir_{c}}(I)
on
H
satisfies
\rho_{I}(U_{0}(\varphi))=U(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in\Diff_{I}(S^{1})
.
The projective actions (
2
) and (
28
) of
\Diff_{I}(S^{1})
on
H
therefore agree.
Taking the colimit over
I
,
the projective actions (
3
) and (
28
) of
\tDiff(S^{1})
on
H
also agree.
Letting
r_{t}:=\exp(it\ell_{0})\in\tRot(S^{1})
be rotation by angle
t
, the desired result follows:

iL_{0}=iT_{H}(1)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\big(\exp(iT_{H}(t))\big)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}U\big(\exp(t\tfrac{d}{d\theta}))\big)=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}U(r_{t}).\qed

By Proposition
4.16
, (
3
) and (
28
),
we have compatible actions
\tDiff(S^{1})\to PU(H)
and
\Vir_{c}\to\End(H^{f.e.})
on any representation
H
of a conformal net.
And by (
23
), the latter integrates to a holomorphic action

\pi:\tAnn_{c}\to B(H)

(30)

extending and lifting the action of
\tDiff(S^{1})
.
When
H=H_{0}
is the vacuum sector of our conformal net, the eigenvalues of
L_{0}
are all integral
(because the homomorphism

\pi:\tMob\to U(H_{0})
descends to
\Mob
),
so the map
\tAnn_{c}\to B(H_{0})
descends to an action

\pi_{0}:\Ann_{c}\to B(H_{0}).

(31)

Let now
I\subset S^{1}
be an interval.
The formula (
23
) for the action of
\Ann_{c}(I)\subset\Ann_{c}
only involves operators affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
.
So the action of
\Ann_{c}(I)
on
H_{0}
lands in the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\subset B(H_{0})
,
and (
31
) restricts to a homomorphism
\pi_{0}:\Ann_{c}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(I)
.
The following was proven in
[
HT25
, Thm. 7.4]
:

Proposition 4.17
.

For every representation
(H,\rho)
of
{\mathcal{A}}
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ann_{c}(I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}(I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{I}}

where

\pi_{0}
and
\pi
are given by (
31
) and (
30
), respectively.

4.4
Unparametrised annuli

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net. As in (
4
), we let
H_{0}(D)=\Conf({\mathbb{D}},D)\times_{\Mob}H_{0}
be the vacuum sector associated to a disc
D
, and recall that
H_{0}=H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
.
If
D_{in}\subset D_{out}
is a pair of complex discs included into one another, with
A:=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
, then there is an associated operator

Y_{A}:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})

(32)

defined as follows.
Any choice of holomorphic parametrizations
\varphi_{in}:{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{in}
and
\varphi_{out}:{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{out}

provides a lift of
A
to an element
f:=\varphi_{out}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{in}\in\Univ
.
Writing
(\varphi,\xi)
for the element previousely denoted
[(\varphi,\xi)]
,
we set

Y_{A}(\varphi_{in},\xi):=(\varphi_{out},\pi(f)\xi),

where
\pi:\Univ\subset\Ann_{c}\to B(H_{0})
is the map (
31
).
To see that the map
Y_{A}
is well defined, we first verify that it is independent of the way one represents an element of
H_{0}(D_{in})
as
(\varphi_{in},\xi)
.
Indeed, for any
\psi\in\Mob
, the two expressions

Y_{A}(\varphi_{in}\circ\psi,\xi)=(\varphi_{out},\pi({f\circ\psi})\xi)

and

Y_{A}(\varphi_{in},\pi(\psi)\xi)=(\varphi_{out},\pi(f)\pi(\psi)\xi)

are equal in
H_{0}(D_{out})
.
We must also check that
Y_{A}
is independent of the choice of parametrization
\varphi_{out}
.
Indeed, had we chosen
\varphi_{out}\circ\psi
instead of
\varphi_{out}
for some
\psi\in\Mob
, we would have obtained the same element of
H_{0}(D_{out})
:

Y_{A}(\varphi_{in},\xi)=(\varphi_{out}\circ\psi,\pi({\psi^{-1}\circ f})\xi)=(\varphi_{out},\pi(f)\xi).

This finishes the proof that the map (
32
) is well defined.
The maps
Y_{A}
are compatible with composition,

Y_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}=Y_{A_{1}}\circ Y_{A_{2}},

making
D\mapsto H_{0}(D)
into a functor from the category of discs (Definition
2.1
) and holomorphic embeddings (a.k.a. univalent maps) to the category of Hilbert spaces and bounded linear maps.

Recall from (
4
) that the vacuum vector
\Omega_{D}\in H_{0}(D)
is the equivalence class of
(\varphi,\Omega)
, for any
\varphi\in\Conf({\mathbb{D}},D)
.
By (
25
), we have:

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
D_{in}\subset D_{out}
be discs,
and let
\Omega_{in/out}:=\Omega_{D_{in/out}}\in H_{0}(D_{in/out})
be their respective vacuum vectors.
Then

\,\,Y_{D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}}\Omega_{in}=\Omega_{out}.\!\!\hskip 179.25244pt\square\hskip-179.25244pt

The operators
Y_{A}
associated to thin annuli will be particularly important for us:

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be a thin annulus, and let
I\subset\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A
be an interval.
Then
Y_{A}
is equivariant for the actions of
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
on
H_{0}(D_{in})
and on
H_{0}(D_{out})
.

Proof.

Recall from (
5
) that the action of
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
on
H_{0}(D_{in})
is given by
x(\varphi_{in},\xi)=(\varphi_{in},{\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)\xi)
, and similarly for
H_{0}(D_{out})
.
Letting
f=\varphi_{out}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{in}
,
we need to show that the following two expressions are equal in
H_{0}(D_{out})
:

\displaystyle xY_{A}(\varphi_{in},\xi)=x(\varphi_{out},\pi(f)\xi)=(\varphi_{out},{\varphi^{-1}_{out}}(x)\pi(f)\xi)

and

\displaystyle Y_{A}x(\varphi_{in},\xi)=Y_{A}(\varphi_{in},{\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)\xi)=(\varphi_{out},\pi(f){\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)\xi).

We check that
{\varphi^{-1}_{out}}(x)\pi(f)=\pi({f}){\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)
.
Let
g\in\Diff_{c}(S^{1})
be an element whose underlying diffeomorphism
g_{0}\in\Diff(S^{1})
satisfies
(g_{0}\circ f)|_{{\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(I)}=\id
,
and let
U_{g}\in U(H_{0})
be the corresponding unitary.
By construction, we have

A_{g}\cup A_{f}\in\Ann_{c}(\varphi^{-1}_{in}(I)^{\prime}),

so
U_{g}\pi(f)=\pi(A_{g}\cup A_{f})
commutes with
\varphi^{-1}_{in}(x)
. It follows that

{\varphi^{-1}_{out}}(x)\pi(f)={\varphi^{-1}_{out}}(x)U_{g}^{*}U_{g}\pi(f)=U_{g}^{*}{\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)U_{g}\pi(f)=U_{g}^{*}U_{g}\pi(f){\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x)=\pi(f){\varphi^{-1}_{in}}(x).

∎

Remark 4.20
.

Note that, by the Reeh-Schlieder theorem, Lemmas
4.18
and
4.19
taken together completely
characterise the operator
Y_{A}:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
for thin annuli.

The operators
Y_{A}
assigned to unparametrised annuli are closely related to the representation
\pi_{0}:\Ann_{c}\to B(H_{0})
described in Section
4.3
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus with boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
(we do not require
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
to extend to holomorphic maps
{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{in/out}
),
and let

U(\varphi_{in}):H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}(D_{in})\qquad\text{and}\qquad U(\varphi_{out}):H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}(D_{out})

be unitaries implementing
\varphi_{in/out}
(Definition
2.5
).
Then for any lift
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
of
(A,\varphi_{in},\varphi_{out})
, the equality

\pi_{0}(\underline{A})=U(\varphi_{out})^{*}Y_{A}U(\varphi_{in})

holds up to scalar.

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
be diffeomorphisms
that extend to holomorphic maps
{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{in/out}
, so that
\underline{A}^{\prime}:=(A,\varphi_{in}^{\prime},\varphi_{out}^{\prime})\in\Univ
.
The unitaries
U(\varphi_{in/out}^{\prime}):H_{0}\to H_{0}(D_{in/out})
defined by
U(\varphi_{in/out}^{\prime})(\xi):=(\varphi_{in/out},\xi)
implement
\varphi_{in/out}
.
And the map
Y_{A}
satisfies

\pi(\underline{A}^{\prime})=U(\varphi_{out}^{\prime})^{*}Y_{A}U(\varphi_{in}^{\prime})

by definition.
Writing

\varphi_{in}=\varphi_{in}^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{in}^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi_{out}=\varphi_{out}^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{out}^{\prime\prime}
,
and letting
U(\varphi_{in/out}^{\prime\prime})
be unitaries implementing
\varphi_{in/out}^{\prime\prime}
,
we then have

\pi(\underline{A})=U(\varphi_{out}^{\prime\prime})^{*}\pi(\underline{A}^{\prime})U(\varphi_{in}^{\prime\prime})=U(\varphi_{out}^{\prime\prime})^{*}U(\varphi_{out}^{\prime})^{*}Y_{A}U(\varphi_{in}^{\prime})U(\varphi_{in}^{\prime\prime})=U(\varphi_{out})^{*}Y_{A}U(\varphi_{in}),

where all the equalities hold up to scalar.
∎

From now on, when no confusion can arise, we shall denote the operator
Y_{A}:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
simply by

A\,:\,H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out}).

(33)

Our next task will be to extend the construction (
32
) to assign anti-linear maps
H_{0}(D_{1})\to H_{0}(D_{2})
to
anti-holomorphic embeddings
D_{1}\to D_{2}
between discs.
Before that, we need some background on the Bisognano-Wichmann theorem
(the reader may consult
[
GF93
, Thm. 2.19]
,
[
BSM90
]
,
[
DLR01
, Thm. 2.1]
,
[
Bor92
, Thm. II.9]
, or
[
Lon08
, Thm. 6.2.3]
).

Recall that
{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the standard unit disc. Let
I_{-}\subset\partial{\mathbb{D}}
be the lower semi-circle, and let
I_{+}:=I_{-}^{\prime}
be its closed complement, so that
\partial I_{-}=\partial I_{+}=\{\pm 1\}
.
Let
\delta:i{\mathbb{R}}\to\Mob=\Aut({\mathbb{D}})
be the unique action of
i{\mathbb{R}}
on
{\mathbb{D}}
that fixes
\{\pm 1\}
and is normalised so that the derivative at
1
of
\delta_{it}
is
e^{t}
.
Finally, let
\vartheta(z)=\bar{z}
.
The Bisognano Wichman theorem for conformal nets says that
H_{0}
is canonically isomorphic to the
L^{2}
-space of the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
, and that the modular flow
\Delta^{it}:L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})\to L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
agrees under that isomorphism with the action on
H_{0}=H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
induced by
\delta_{2\pi it}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
.
Moreover, the modular conjugation
J:L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})\to L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
corresponds to an operator

\Theta:H_{0}\to H_{0}

(34)

that satisfies
\Theta{\mathcal{A}}(I)\Theta={\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta I)
for every interval
I\subset S^{1}
.
By
[
BDH15
, Prop. 4.7]
, the actions of
\Theta
and
\Diff_{c}(S^{1})
on
H_{0}
assemble to an action of the semidirect product
\Diff_{c}(S^{1})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
By differentiating the
1
-parameter groups of unitaries

\displaystyle\Theta\exp(it(L_{n}+L_{-n}))\Theta

\displaystyle=\exp(-it(L_{n}+L_{-n}))

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\,\,\,\Theta\exp(t(L_{n}-L_{-n}))\Theta

\displaystyle=\exp(t(L_{n}-L_{-n}))

and using that
\Theta
is conjugate linear, we learn that

\Theta(\lambda L_{n})\Theta=\bar{\lambda}L_{n}\qquad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

(35)

It follows by continuity that

\Theta T_{H_{0}}(f)\Theta=T_{H_{0}}(f^{\vartheta})

(36)

for all
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(S^{1})
, where
f^{\vartheta}(\theta):=\overline{f(-\theta)}
.
Combining (
36
) with the explicit formula (
23
) for the action
of
\Ann_{c}
, we see that the actions of
\Theta
and
\Ann_{c}
on
H_{0}
assemble to an action

\pi:\Ann_{c}\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2\to B(H_{0}).

(37)

Remark 4.22
.

The semigroup
\Ann\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2
is the set of isomorphism classes of
unoriented annuli with parametrised boundary
,
and its subgroup

{\Diff(S^{1})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2}
is the group of all diffeomorphisms of the circle (not necessarily orientation preserving).
Here,
unoriented annuli
are locally ringed spaces
A=(A,{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
which are isomorphic to embedded annuli, as in Definition
4.1
,
with the only difference being that we now treat
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as a sheaf of
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebras as opposed to a sheaf of
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebras.
Additionally, the boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in}:S^{1}\to\partial_{in}A
and
\varphi_{out}:S^{1}\to\partial_{out}A
are no longer required to satisfy any orientation conditions.

The semigroup of unoriented annuli with parametrised boundary admits an extension by
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}

0\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to\Ann_{c}\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2\to\Ann\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2\to 0

but this is not a central extension, because
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts non-trivially on the center
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
of
\Ann_{c}
.

We can now return to the task of extending the construction (
32
) to assign anti-linear maps to anti-holomorphic embeddings between discs.
Given two discs
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
and
an embedding
f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
which is either holomorphic or anti-holomorphic, we have an associated linear or anti-linear map
f_{*}:H_{0}(D_{1})\to H_{0}(D_{2})
. It is given by

f_{*}(\varphi_{1},\xi):=(\varphi_{2},\pi(\varphi_{2}^{-1}\circ f\circ\varphi_{1})\xi),

(38)

where
\varphi_{i}:{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{i}
are holomorphic parametrisations,

\varphi_{2}^{-1}\circ f\circ\varphi_{1}\in\Univ\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2\subset\Ann_{c}\rtimes\,{\mathbb{Z}}/2
, and
\pi
is as in (
37
).
This construction extends (
32
), and is well defined for the same reasons.
It is functorial in the sense that given
f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
and
g:D_{2}\to D_{3}
, we have

g_{*}\circ f_{*}=(g\circ f)_{*}:H_{0}(D_{1})\to H_{0}(D_{3}).

In summary, our construction equips the assignment
D\mapsto H_{0}(D)
with the structure of a functor from the category of discs and embeddings that are either holomorphic or anti-holomorphic, to the category of Hilbert spaces and bounded maps that either complex linear or complex anti-linear.

5
Worm-shaped insertions

Given a vertex algebra
V=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
, a collection
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
of distinct points in the complex plane, and elements
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
, we can form the element

Y(v_{1},z_{1})\ldots Y(v_{n},z_{n})\Omega

of the algebraic completion
\widehat{V}:=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
.
More generally, if we’re also given
local coordinates

g_{i}\in\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
at the points
z_{i}
,
we can form the element

Y(g_{1}(v_{1}),z_{1})\ldots Y(g_{n}(v_{n}),z_{n})\Omega\,\in\,\widehat{V}.

(39)

If the VOA is moreover unitary, and the points are in
\mathring{\mathbb{D}}
(i.e., satisfy
|z_{i}|<1
),
then the above element (
39
) lies in fact in the Hilbert space completion of
V
.
In this section, we study an analogous construction which takes place in the world of conformal nets.
The
z_{i}
and
g_{i}

are replaced by disjoint intervals
I_{i}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
, and the
v_{i}
are replaced by elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
of the conformal net.

5.1
Construction of worm-shaped insertion

Definition 5.1
.

Let
D
be a disc.
An embedded interval
I\subset D
is called
extendable
if there exists a larger interval
I_{+}\subset D

containing
I
in its interior, and if the orientations of
I
and
\partial D
agree whenever the two intersect (they are necessarily tangent at such points).

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net, and let
H_{0}
be its vacuum sector.
In this section, for every collection of disjoint oriented extendable intervals
I_{i}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
, and elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
, we will construct a vector

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle\in H_{0}
.
More generally, for any disc
D
, disjoint oriented extendable intervals
I_{i}\subset D
, and elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
, we will construct a corresponding vector

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D).

These vectors are completely determined by the following two requirements:

•

If all
the intervals
I_{i}
lie in the boundary of the disc, then

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\Omega_{D}\in H_{0}(D),

(40)

where the right hand side uses the actions (
5
) of the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
on
H_{0}(D)
.
(And the disc with no insertions corresponds to the vacuum vector.)

•

If
D_{in}\subset D_{out}
are discs, and
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is the corresponding annlus, then

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D_{out}}=Y_{A}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D_{in}}.

Given a disc
D
,
disjoint oriented extendable intervals
I_{i}\subset D

(the intervals
I_{i}
are allowed to touch the boundary of
D
),
and elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
, pick a sequence of discs
15
15

15

If the intersections
I_{i}\cap\partial D
have multiple connected components, such a sequence of discs might only exist after permuting the order of the intervals
I_{i}
.

D_{1}\subset D_{2}\subset\ldots\subset D_{n}=D

(41)

with
I_{i}\subset\partial D_{i}
(compatibly with the orientations), and let
A_{i}:=D_{i}\setminus D_{i-1}
:

{\scriptstyle x_{1}}
{\scriptstyle x_{2}}
{\scriptstyle x_{3}}
{\scriptstyle x_{4}}
D_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{4}
A_{5}

Letting
\Omega_{1}\in H_{0}(D_{1})
be the vacuum vector associated to
D_{1}
,
we set

|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}\,:=\,A_{n}x_{n-1}\cdots A_{3}x_{2}A_{2}x_{1}\Omega_{1}

(42)

where
A_{j}
denotes the operator
Y_{A_{j}}:H_{0}(D_{i-1})\to H_{0}(D_{i})
, as in (
33
).
In Proposition
5.2
below, we will show that the above vector is independent of the choice of discs
D_{i}
and of the order of the insertions.
The subscript in the left hand side of (
42
) emphasizes the potential dependence on these choices.
(After Proposition
5.2
has been proven we will drop that subscript and replace it by the subscript
D
.)

Suppose now that we are given an annulus
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
with disjoin oriented extendable intervals
I_{i}\subset A
, and elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
. Let

D_{in}=D_{0}\subset D_{1}\subset\ldots\subset D_{n+1}=D_{out}

(43)

be a sequence of discs
16
16

16

Once again, if
I_{i}\cap\partial A
have multiple connected components, then such a sequence of discs might only exist after permuting the order of the
I_{i}
’s.
with
I_{i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, and let
A_{i}:=D_{i}\setminus D_{i-1}
:

{\scriptstyle x_{1}}
{\scriptstyle x_{2}}
{\scriptstyle x_{3}}
A_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{4}

Then we may form the operator

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}:=A_{n+1}x_{n}\cdots x_{2}A_{2}x_{1}A_{1}:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out}).

(44)

where
A_{j}=Y_{A_{j}}
as in (
33
).

Given boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}
of
A
, a
z
-lift
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
, and a representation
K
of the conformal net,
we can also form the operator

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}\,:=\,\underline{A}_{n+1}x_{n}\cdots x_{2}\underline{A}_{2}x_{1}\underline{A}_{1}:K\to K.

(45)

Here,
\underline{A}_{j}\in\tAnn_{c}
are
z
-lifts of the annuli
A_{j}
which appear in (
44
), subject to the constraint
\prod\underline{A}_{j}=\underline{A}
, and
the parametrisation
\varphi_{j}
of
\partial_{out}A_{j}
is required to agree with that of
\partial_{in}A_{j+1}
.
The elements
x_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})
act on
K
by means of the homomorphism

\varphi_{j}^{*}:{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})\to{\mathcal{A}}(\varphi_{j}^{-1}(I_{j}))
.

Note that if
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}(I)
,
K=H_{0}
, and the intervals
I_{i}
are disjoint from
\varphi_{in}(I^{\prime})=\varphi_{out}(I^{\prime})
, then provided we make suitable choices of intermediate parametrizations, we have

\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)

(46)

by construction.

When
K=H_{0}
, we may compare (
44
) and (
45
), as follows.
Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
and
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
be as in Lemma
4.21
, and let
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
be a lift of
(A,\varphi_{in},\varphi_{out})
.
By Lemma
4.21
, we may choose unitaries
U(\varphi_{in/out}):H_{0}\to H_{0}(D_{in/out})
implementing
{\varphi_{in/out}}
, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
such that
\underline{A}=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}AU(\varphi_{in})
.
We then claim that the same relation also holds in the presence of worm insertions:

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}U(\varphi_{in}).

(47)

To see that, choose unitaries
U(\varphi_{j})
implementing the intermediate parametrizations
\varphi_{j}
.
Since
\underline{A}=\prod\underline{A}_{j}
, we may choose these unitaries so as to have
\underline{A}_{i}=U(\varphi_{i})^{*}A_{i}U(\varphi_{i-1})
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
, and

\underline{A}_{n+1}=z\cdot U(\varphi_{n+1})^{*}A_{n+1}U(\varphi_{n})

(where by convention
\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{in}
and
\varphi_{n+1}=\varphi_{out}
).
We can then check that the relation (
47
) indeed holds:

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}

\displaystyle=\underline{A}_{n+1}\varphi_{n}^{*}(x_{n})\underline{A}_{n}\cdots\varphi_{1}^{*}(x_{1})\underline{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=[z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A_{n+1}U(\varphi_{n})]\varphi_{n}^{*}(x_{n})[U(\varphi_{n})^{*}A_{n}U(\varphi_{n-1})]\cdots\varphi_{1}^{*}(x_{1})[U(\varphi_{1})^{*}A_{1}U(\varphi_{in})]

\displaystyle=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A_{n+1}x_{n}A_{n-1}\cdots x_{1}A_{1}U(\varphi_{in})

\displaystyle=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}U(\varphi_{in}).

Our next goal is to show that (
42
), (
44
), and (
45
) are independent of the choices of discs and ordering of the insertions.
In the proof of Proposition
5.2
below, we first assume that
I_{i}\cap\partial D
and
I_{i}\cap\partial A
are either empty or connected.
This ensures that sequences of discs as in (
41
) or (
43
) always exist when needed.
We then explain, at the end of the proof, how to remove that constraint.

Recall that an annulus
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is called ‘thin’ if
\partial D_{out}\cap\partial D_{in}
contains an interval.

Proposition 5.2
.

(​​​​
a​​
)​​

The vector (
42
) corresponding to a disc with worm insertions is independent of the choice of intermediate discs
D_{i}
, and of the ordering of the insertions.

(​​​​
b​​
)​​

The operator (
44
) corresponding to an unparametrised annulus with worm insertions is independent of the choice of intermediate discs
D_{i}
, and of the ordering of the insertions.

(​​​​
c​​
)​​

For every representation
K
of
{\mathcal{A}}
, the operator (
45
) on
K
corresponding to a
z
-lifted parametrised annulus with worm insertions is independent of the choice of intermediate discs
D_{i}
, and of the ordering of the insertions.

In light of Proposition
5.2
, we will later simplify the notations
(
42
), (
44
), (
45
) by simply writing

|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}

in place of
|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}
,

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]

in place of
A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}
,
and

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]

in place of
\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}
.
Note that, by construction, if
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is an annulus, then we have

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]|y_{1}y_{2}\ldots y_{m}\rangle_{D_{in}}=|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}y_{1}y_{2}\ldots y_{m}\rangle_{D_{out}}.

(48)

Proof.

As mentioned above, we start by assuming that the intersections
I_{i}\cap\partial D
and
I_{i}\cap\partial A
are either empty or connected.

Our proof is by induction, broken into four steps.
Our base case is the statement
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
for
n=0
, in which case there is nothing to show.
In
Step 1
of our inductive proof, we show that if
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holds whenever there are
n
insertions (for
n\geq 0
) then

(\ref{item 1 independence : discs})
holds whenever there are
n+1
insertions.
In
Step 2
, we show that if
(\ref{item 1 independence : discs})
holds whenever there are
n
insertions for some
n\geq 1
, then
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holds whenever there are
n
insertions and the annulus
A
is thin.
In
Step 3
, we show that
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
for thin annuli with
n
insertions implies the corresponding parametrised version
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})
, again just for thin annuli.
Finally, in
Step 4
, we show that the statements
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
and
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})

for thin annuli imply the corresponding statements without the restriction of the annuli being thin.

Step 1.

In this step, assuming that
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holds when there are
n
insertions, we show that
(\ref{item 1 independence : discs})
holds when there are
n+1
insertions.
Let
D
be a disc, let
I_{0},\ldots,I_{n}\subset D
be
disjoint oriented extendable intervals,
and let
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
. Given
discs
D_{0}\subset\ldots\subset D_{n}=D
such that
I_{i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, we can form

|x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}\in H_{0}(D)
as in (
42
).
Let
\sigma
be a permutation of the set
\{0,\ldots,n\}
,
let
D^{\prime}_{0}\subset\ldots\subset D^{\prime}_{n}=D
be such that
I_{\sigma(i)}\subset\partial D^{\prime}_{i}
,
and let

|x_{\sigma(0)}x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}\in H_{0}(D)
be the corresponding element.
We wish to show that

|x_{\sigma(0)}x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}=|x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}
.

If
\sigma(0)=0
, pick a disc
D^{\prime\prime}_{0}\subset D_{0}\cap D^{\prime}_{0}
that contains
I_{0}
in its boundary, and let
\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\in H_{0}(D^{\prime\prime}_{0})
be the corresponding vacuum vector.
Using Lemmas
4.18
and
4.19
,
we then check:

\displaystyle|x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}_{0})[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]x_{0}(D_{0}\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}_{0})[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}](D_{0}\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})x_{0}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]x_{0}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}},

and similarly

\displaystyle|x_{\sigma(0)}x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\sigma(1)}x_{\sigma(2)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}]x_{0}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]x_{0}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}},

where the last equality follows from our inductive assumption about
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holding whenever there are
n
insertions.
It follows that

|x_{\sigma(0)}x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}=|x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}.

(49)

If
\sigma(0)\not=0
, pick permutations
\pi
and
\tau
of
\{0,\ldots,n\}
that satisfy
\pi(0)=\tau(1)=0
and
\pi(1)=\tau(0)=\sigma(0)
.
Let
D^{\prime\prime}_{0}\subset D
be a disc that contains both
I_{0}
and
I_{\sigma(0)}
in its boundary, and that doesn’t intersect any of the other intervals, and let
\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\in H_{0}(D^{\prime\prime}_{0})
be the associated vacuum vector.
We then have:

\displaystyle|x_{\sigma(0)}x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\sigma(1)}x_{\sigma(2)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}]x_{\sigma(0)}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}
