\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\tau(1)}x_{\tau(2)}\ldots x_{\tau(n)}]x_{\tau(0)}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\tau(2)}\ldots x_{\tau(n)}]x_{\tau(1)}x_{\tau(0)}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\pi(2)}\ldots x_{\pi(n)}]x_{\pi(1)}x_{\pi(0)}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{\pi(1)}x_{\pi(2)}\ldots x_{\pi(n)}]x_{\pi(0)}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime\prime}_{0})[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]x_{0}\Omega_{D^{\prime\prime}_{0}}

\displaystyle=|x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})},

where the first and last equalities are special cases of (
49
).
The second, fourth, and sixth equalities hold by our inductive assumption about
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holding when there are
\leq n
insertions, while the fourth equality also uses the locality axiom to argue that
x_{\tau(1)}x_{\tau(0)}=x_{\pi(1)}x_{\pi(0)}
.

Step 2.

In this step, assuming that
(\ref{item 1 independence : discs})
holds with
n
insertions (
n\geq 1
),
we show that
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holds for thin annuli with the same number of insertions.
Let
D_{in}\subset D_{out}
be discs such that
\partial D_{out}\cap\partial D_{in}
contains an interval
J
, and let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be the corresponding thin annulus.
Let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}\subset A\setminus J
be disjoint oriented extendable intervals, and let
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
.

Given a family of discs

D_{in}=D_{0}\subset\ldots\subset D_{n+1}=D_{out}

such that
I_{i}\subset\partial D_{i}
,
let

X_{(D_{j})}:=A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})

be as in (
44
).
Given a permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and discs
D_{in}=D^{\prime}_{0}\subset\ldots\subset D^{\prime}_{n+1}=D_{out}
such that
I_{\sigma(i)}\subset\partial D^{\prime}_{i}
,
we wish to show that
X_{(D_{j})}=X_{(D_{j}^{\prime})}:=A[x_{\sigma(1)}x_{\sigma(2)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}]_{(D^{\prime}_{j})}
.
By our induction hypothesis, we have

X_{(D_{j})}\Omega_{D_{in}}=|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{(D_{j})}=|x_{\sigma(1)}x_{\sigma(2)}\ldots x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle_{(D_{j}^{\prime})}=X_{(D_{j}^{\prime})}\Omega_{D_{in}}.

The maps
X_{(D_{j})}
and
X_{(D_{j}^{\prime})}
are
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
-linear by Lemma
4.19
, and
the vacuum vector
\Omega_{D_{in}}\in H_{0}(D_{in})
is cyclic for the action of
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
by the Reeh-Schlieder theorem. It follows that
X_{(D_{j})}=X_{(D_{j}^{\prime})}
.

Step 3.

In this step we assume that
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
holds for thin annuli with
n
insertions (
n\geq 1
), and show that
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})
holds for thin annuli with the same number of insertions.

Let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
be a thin
z
-lifted annulus, with underlying annulus
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
,
and boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
.
Let
J\subset\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A
be an interval,

I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}\subset A\setminus J
be disjoint oriented extendable intervals, and
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
.
Choose discs
D_{in}=D_{0}\subset D_{1}\subset\ldots\subset D_{n+1}=D_{out}

such that
I_{i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, as well as parametrizations
\varphi_{i}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Equip
A_{i}:=D_{i}\setminus\mathring{D}_{i-1}
with the boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{i-1}
and
\varphi_{i}
(where by convention
\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{in}
and
\varphi_{n+1}=\varphi_{out}
), and choose
z
-lifts
\underline{A}_{i}\in\tAnn_{c}
of
(A_{i},\varphi_{i-1},\varphi_{i})
satisfying
\prod\underline{A}_{j}=\underline{A}
.
For every representation
K
of the conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, we then have an operator

X_{(D_{j},\varphi_{j})}:=\underline{A}_{n+1}\varphi_{n}^{*}(x_{n})\cdots\underline{A}_{3}\varphi_{2}^{*}(x_{2})\underline{A}_{2}\varphi_{1}^{*}(x_{1})\underline{A}_{1}:K\to K,

(50)

as in (
45
).
We need to show that this operator is independent of the choices of ordering of the intervals, intermediate discs, intermediate parametrizations
\varphi_{j}
, and lifts
\underline{A}_{j}
.

If
K=H_{0}
is the vacuum sector, then by (
47
) and our assumption
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
we have

X_{(D_{j},\varphi_{j})}=U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]U(\varphi_{in}),

(51)

which is visibly independent of the choices.

Returning to the case of an arbitrary sector
K
, we start by showing that
X_{(D_{j},\varphi_{j})}
does not depend on the choice of intermediate parametrizations
\varphi_{j}
.
Given new such parametrizations
\varphi_{j}^{\prime}:S^{1}\to D_{j}
, let
\alpha_{j}:=\varphi^{-1}_{j}\circ\varphi^{\prime}_{j}\in\Diff(S^{1})
.
Pick
z
-lifts
\underline{A}_{j}^{\prime}
,
\underline{\alpha}_{j}\in\tAnn_{c}
of
(A_{j},\varphi^{\prime}_{j-1},\varphi^{\prime}_{j})
and
\alpha_{j}

subject to the condition

\underline{A}_{j}^{\prime}=\underline{\alpha}_{j}^{-1}\underline{A}_{j}\,\underline{\alpha}_{j-1}

(by convention,
\underline{\alpha}_{0}
and
\underline{\alpha}_{n+1}
are taken to be the identity).
We then have
\prod\underline{A}^{\prime}_{j}=\underline{A}
, and we show by direct computation
that (
50
) does not depend on the intermediate parametrizations or the individual lifts
\underline{A}_{j}
:

\displaystyle X_{(D_{j},\varphi_{j}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\underline{A}_{n+1}\underline{\alpha}_{n}\varphi_{n}^{\prime*}(x_{n})\underline{\alpha}_{n}^{-1}\underline{A}_{n}\,\underline{\alpha}_{n-1}\cdots\underline{\alpha}_{1}\varphi_{1}^{\prime*}(x_{1})\underline{\alpha}_{1}^{-1}\underline{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{A}_{n+1}\varphi_{n}^{*}(x_{n})\underline{A}_{n}\cdots\varphi_{1}^{*}(x_{1})\underline{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=X_{(D_{j},\varphi_{j})}.

In light of the above, from now on, we denote this operator simply by
X_{(D_{j})}
.

We now show that
X_{(D_{j})}
does not depend on the choices of discs
D_{j}
.
We first work under the additional assumption that
\underline{A}
is localised in

(\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime}
, i.e., that

\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}((\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime})\subset\tAnn_{c}

(which in particular implies
\varphi_{in}|_{\varphi_{in}^{-1}J}=\varphi_{out}|_{\varphi_{in}^{-1}J}
).
Without loss of generality, we may insist that the intermediate parametrizations
\varphi_{j}
are chosen so that
\varphi_{in}=\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n+1}=\varphi_{out}
all agree on
\varphi_{in}^{-1}J
, and that the
z
-lifts

\underline{A}_{j}
are localised in

(\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime}
.
Write
\pi:{\mathcal{A}}((\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime})\to B(K)
for the action of the conformal net, and let
X^{0}
be the operator on
H_{0}
defined by the same formula (
45
).
We showed in (
51
) that
X^{0}
does not depend on the choices of discs.
By Proposition
4.17
and the fact that
\varphi_{j}^{*}(x_{j})\in{\mathcal{A}}((\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime})
, we then have

\pi(X^{0})=X_{(D_{j})},

from which we can infer that
X_{(D_{j})}
also doesn’t depend on the choice of discs
D_{j}
.

Finally, we drop the assumption that
\underline{A}
is localised in some interval.
Since
A
is thin, we may pick a lifted diffeomorphism
\underline{\beta}\in\tDiff_{c}(S^{1})
such that
\underline{\beta}\underline{A}
is localised in

(\varphi_{in}^{-1}J)^{\prime}
.
Let
X_{\underline{\beta}\underline{A}}
be the operator (
50
) corresponding to
\underline{\beta}\underline{A}
.
By the results of the previous paragraph,

X_{\underline{\beta}\underline{A}}

does not depend on choice of intermediate discs.
It follows that

X_{(D_{j})}=\underline{\beta}^{-1}X_{\underline{\beta}\underline{A}}
also
doesn’t depend on choice of intermediate discs.

Step 4.

In this step, we show that if
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
and
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})
hold for thin annuli with
n
insertions, then they hold for all annuli with that same number
n
of insertions.
We treat in parallel the following two cases which correspond, respectively, to
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
and
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})
:

Case (I):
annuli with unparametrised boundary (acting on vacuum sectors only), and

Case (II):

z
-lifted annuli with parametrised boundary, acting an arbitrary sectors.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus D_{in}
be an annulus with
n
insertions along intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}
. [In case (II), we further equip
A
with boundary parametrisations, and a lift to the central extension
\tAnn_{c}
.]
Our first task is to produce an operator
A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{\sim}
, not necessarily equal to
A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}
, but independent of any choices. Later we will show that these two operators are in fact equal to each other.

Consider the quotient space
A/{\sim}
obtained by crushing each of the intervals
I_{i}
to a single point
p_{i}\in A/{\sim}
. The annulus
A/{\sim}
is a priori not a smooth manifold (it’s certainly not a complex manifold), but we may pick a smooth structure extending the one given on
A\setminus\bigcup I_{i}
. Given a subspace
X\subset A/{\sim}
, we write
\tilde{X}
for its preimage in
A
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
denote the space of possible decompositions
A/{\sim}=B\cup C
into two annuli with thin parts.
[In case (II), both
\tilde{B}
and
\tilde{C}
are equipped with boundary parametrisations, and lifts to
\tAnn_{c}
, composing to the given lift of
A
.]
The space
{\mathcal{D}}
contains a subspace
\mathring{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{D}}
of decompositions such that the insertion points are not on the boundary of
B
or
C
.
For
(B,C)\in\mathring{\mathcal{D}}
, the annuli
\tilde{B}
and
\tilde{C}
have smooth boundary, and we set

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{\sim}:=\tilde{B}[\ldots]\tilde{C}[\ldots]

(52)

where the unspecified dots refer to the insertions that lie inside
B
and inside
C
, respectively.

Let us define
(B,C)
and
(B^{\prime},C^{\prime})
in
\mathring{\mathcal{D}}
to be
close enough
, if there exists
(B^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})\in\mathring{\mathcal{D}}
such that
B^{\prime\prime}\subset B\cap B^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}\supset C\cup C^{\prime}
.
In that case, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{B}[\ldots]\tilde{C}[\ldots]

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}^{\prime\prime}[\ldots](\tilde{B}\setminus\tilde{B}^{\prime\prime})[\ldots]\tilde{C}[\ldots]

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}^{\prime\prime}[\ldots]\tilde{C}^{\prime\prime}[\ldots]

(53)

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}^{\prime\prime}[\ldots](\tilde{B}^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{B}^{\prime\prime})[\ldots]\tilde{C}^{\prime}[\ldots]=\tilde{B}^{\prime}[\ldots]\tilde{C}^{\prime}[\ldots]

As a consequence of the existence of framings (
[
HT24
, Corollary 4.17]
),
the space
{\mathcal{D}}
is path connected. So any two decompositions
A/{\sim}=B_{0}\cup C_{0}
and
A/{\sim}=B_{1}\cup C_{1}
can be connected by a smooth path

A/{\sim}=B_{t}\cup C_{t}\qquad t\in[0,1],

which we may furthermore assume to be transverse to the insertion points
p_{i}
.
Pick
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{m}=1
such that
(B_{t_{i}},C_{t_{i}})
and
(B_{t_{i+1}},C_{t_{i+1}})
are close enough,
and such that
(B_{t_{i}},C_{t_{i}})\in\mathring{\mathcal{D}}
.
It follows from (
53
) that

\tilde{B}_{0}[\ldots]\tilde{C}_{0}[\ldots]=\tilde{B}_{1}[\ldots]\tilde{C}_{1}[\ldots].

This finishes the proof that
(
52
) is independent of the choice of point in
\mathring{\mathcal{D}}
.

It remains to show that (
52
) is equal to
A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]_{(D_{j})}
. This is obvious in the case
n=1
of a single insertion.
So it’s enough to prove that for every decomposition

A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}
into two annuli (not necessarily thin), compatible with the insertions, we have

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{\sim}=A_{1}[\ldots]^{\sim}A_{2}[\ldots]^{\sim}.

Pick thin annuli
B_{1},B_{2},B^{\prime}_{2},C_{1},C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}\subset A
satisfying

A_{1}=B_{1}C_{1}
,

A_{2}=B_{2}\cup C_{2}
,

\mathring{B}_{2}=\mathring{B}^{\prime}_{2}
,

\mathring{C}_{1}=\mathring{C}^{\prime}_{1}
,
and such that
C_{1}\cup B_{2}=B^{\prime}_{2}\cup C^{\prime}_{1}
is also thin.
[In case (I), we also require
B_{2}=B^{\prime}_{2}
and
C_{1}=C^{\prime}_{1}
as elements of
\tAnn_{c}
.]
Here’s a typical example of what these annuli might look like:

A

A_{1}

A_{2}

B_{1}

B_{2}

B^{\prime}_{2}

C_{1}

C^{\prime}_{1}

C_{2}

Letting
B:=B_{1}B^{\prime}_{2}
and

C:=C^{\prime}_{1}C_{2}
,
we then have
A=B\cup C
, and therefore:

\displaystyle A_{1}[\ldots]^{\sim}A_{2}[\ldots]^{\sim}

\displaystyle=B_{1}[\ldots]C_{1}[\ldots]B_{2}[\ldots]\circ C_{2}[\ldots]

\displaystyle=B_{1}[\ldots]B^{\prime}_{2}[\ldots]C^{\prime}_{1}[\ldots]C_{2}[\ldots]=B[\ldots]C[\ldots]=A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{\sim}.

This finishes the proof of Step 4.

To finish the proof of Proposition
5.2
, we must still treat the case when the intervals
I_{i}
touch the boundary in more than one connected component:

(\ref{item 1 independence : discs})
Let

|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(1)}

and

|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(2)}

be two instances of (
42
),
defined with different choices of intermediate discs and orderings of the insertions.
If
A
is a thick annulus with
\partial_{in}A=\partial D
,
then

Y_{A}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(1)}=|x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\rangle_{A\cup D}^{(1)}=|x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\rangle_{A\cup D}^{(2)}=Y_{A}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(2)},

where the middle equality holds by the work we have done (as the intervals
I_{i}
do not touch the boundary of
A\cup D
).
By Lemmas
4.15
and
4.21
, the operator
Y_{A}
is injective.
It follows that
|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(1)}=|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}^{(2)}
.

(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
, and
let

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{(1)}

and

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]^{(2)}

be two instances of (
44
),
defined with different choices of intermediate discs and orderings of the insertions.
Pick thick annuli
A_{1}
with
\partial_{in}A_{1}=\partial_{out}A
, and
A_{2}\subset D_{in}
with

\partial_{out}A_{2}=\partial_{in}A
.
We then have:

A_{1}A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]^{(1)}A_{2}=(A_{1}AA_{2})[x_{1}\cdots x_{n}]^{(1)}=(A_{1}AA_{2})[x_{1}\cdots x_{n}]^{(2)}=A_{1}A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]^{(2)}A_{2},

where the middle equality holds
as the intervals
I_{i}
do not touch the boundary of
A_{1}\cup A\cup A_{2}
.
By Lemmas
4.15
and
4.21
, the operator
Y_{A_{1}}
and
Y_{A_{2}}
(denoted above simply
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, following our convention (
33
)) are injective with dense image.
It follows that
A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]^{(1)}=A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]^{(2)}
.

The last case,
(\ref{item 3 independence : annuli})
, is established like
(\ref{item 2 independence : annuli})
, by an analogous application of Lemma
4.15
.
∎

We finish this section by collecting a couple of useful lemmas.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus equipped
with boundaries parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
, and let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
be a
z
-lift of
A
.
Pick unitaries
U(\varphi_{in/out}):H_{0}\to H_{0}(D_{in/out})
implementing
\varphi_{in/out}
, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, satisfying

\underline{A}=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}AU(\varphi_{in})

(such unitaries and scalar exist by Lemma
4.21
).
Then the same relation also holds in the presence of worm insertions:

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]U(\varphi_{in}).

Proof.

This relation was established in (
47
), on out way towards proving that the operators
A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]
and
\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]
are well-defined.
∎

The following is a variant of
(
48
):

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
be a univalent map, let
\underline{A}_{f}\in\Univ\subset\Ann_{c}
be the corresponding lifted annulus, and
let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
be worm insertions with support intervals in
A_{f}
.
Then

\underline{A}_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]\big(|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\big)=|y_{1}\ldots y_{m}\,{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}.

Proof.

Let us write

A_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]:H_{0}(f({\mathbb{D}}))\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
and

\underline{A}_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]:H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})

for the operators defined in
(
44
) and
(
45
), respectively (using the embedding
\Univ\hookrightarrow\Ann_{c}
for the latter).
If
U_{f}:H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}(f({\mathbb{D}}))
is the unitary induced by
f
then,
by Lemma
4.21
, the operators
A_{f}:H_{0}(f({\mathbb{D}}))\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})

and
\underline{A}_{f}:H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})

satisfy

\underline{A}_{f}=A_{f}U_{f}

(54)

up to scalar. And as both sides fix the vacuum vector
\Omega\in H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
, the identity (
54
) in fact holds on the nose.
It follows by Lemma
5.3
that

\underline{A}_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]=A_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]U_{f}.

Since (
42
) is functorial with respect to isomorphisms, we have

U_{f}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}=|{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n})\rangle_{f({\mathbb{D}})}.

Hence, by (
48
), we have

\displaystyle\underline{A}_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=A_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]U_{f}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=A_{f}[y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]\,|{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n})\rangle_{f({\mathbb{D}})}

\displaystyle=|y_{1}\ldots y_{m}\,{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.\qed

Our next result is an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.19
:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be a thin annulus, and let
I\subset\partial_{in}A\cap\partial_{out}A
be an interval in the thin part of
A
.
Then if the support intervals of the worms are disjoint from
I
, the operator

A[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})

is equivariant for the actions of
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
on
H_{0}(D_{in})
and on
H_{0}(D_{out})
.

\square

The reader might have noticed that when
A
is a thin annulus, we have used the notation
\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]
to refer to two potentially different things.
The following lemma shows that there is in fact no problem:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
be localised in an interval
I
, and suppose that the support intervals of the worms are disjoint from the image of
I^{\prime}
in
A
. Then the operator

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]:H_{0}\to H_{0}

(55)

lies in
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\subset B(H_{0})
.

Moreover, if
(K,\rho)
is a representation of the conformal net, then the operator

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}]:K\to K

(56)

(defined in (
45
)) is the image of (
55
) under the homomorphism
\rho_{I}:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to B(K)
.

Proof.

The first statement follows from (
46
).
The second statement is immediate from Proposition
4.17
, and the respective definitions of (
55
) and (
56
).
∎

We now describe the adjoint of the operator
\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}]:K\to K
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be a collection of worm insertions.
Then

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}]^{*}=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}\cdots x_{n}^{*}].

Proof.

Decompose
\underline{A}=\underline{A}_{0}\underline{A}_{1}\cdots\underline{A}_{n}
with the interval labelled by
x_{j}
on the outgoing boundary of
A_{j}
.
We then have

\underline{A}[x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}]^{*}=\big(\underline{A}_{0}x_{1}\underline{A}_{1}x_{2}\cdots\underline{A}_{n}\big)^{*}=\underline{A}_{n}^{\dagger}\cdots x_{2}^{*}\underline{A}_{1}^{\dagger}x_{1}^{*}\underline{A}_{0}^{\dagger}=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[x_{1}^{*}x_{2}^{*}\cdots x_{n}^{*}].

∎

5.2
Holomorphicity of worm insertions

Let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}
be abstract intervals (not embedded in any disc or annulus), and let
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
be algebra elements.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
A:=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus,
let
M
be a finite dimensional complex manifold, and let
\alpha_{i}:M\times I_{i}\to\mathring{A}
be smooth maps which are pointwise holomorphic in the sense that
\forall q\in I_{i}

\alpha_{i}|_{M\times\{q\}}:M\to\mathring{A}

is holomorphic.
Let us further assume that for every point
m\in M
, the the maps
\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}}
are embeddings with disjoint images.
For
m\in M
, let
I_{i}(m):=\alpha_{i}(\{m\}\times I_{i})
, and let

x_{i}(m):=(\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}})_{*}(x_{i})\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i}(m)).

Then the map

M\to{\mathcal{B}}\big(H_{0}(D_{in}),H_{0}(D_{out})\big):m\mapsto A[x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]

(57)

is holomorphic.

Similarly, if we have a
z
-lift
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
of
A
, and a representation
K
of the conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, then the map

M\to{\mathcal{B}}(K):m\mapsto\underline{A}[a_{1}(m)\ldots a_{n}(m)]

(58)

is holomorphic.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.3
, we have
\underline{A}[x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]=z\,U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]U(\varphi_{in})
for suitable unitaries
U(\varphi_{in})
and
U(\varphi_{out})
, and scalar
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
which does not depend on
m
.
So (
57
) follows from (
58
), and
we only need to consider the parametrised case.

The result being local in
M
, we may assume without loss of generality that
I_{i}(m)\cap I_{j}(m^{\prime})=\emptyset
for every
m,m^{\prime}\in M
and every
i\not=j
.
Choose a sequence of discs

D_{in}=D_{0}\subset D_{1}\subset\cdots\subset D_{n}=D_{out}

such that
I_{i}(m)\subset\mathring{D}_{i}\setminus D_{i-1}\,\,\forall m\in M
.
Let
A_{i}=D_{i}\setminus\mathring{D}_{i-1}
,
and let
\underline{A}_{i}\in\tAnn_{c}
be compatible
z
-lifts satisfying
\underline{A}=\underline{A}_{1}\ldots\underline{A}_{n}
. Then we have:

\underline{A}[x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]=\underline{A}_{1}[x_{1}(m)]\ldots\underline{A}_{n}[x_{n}(m)].

We are thus reduced to proving the result for a single worm in an annulus.
Write
x
for
x_{1}
, and
I
for
I_{1}
.
Let us also fix an embedding
A\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
.

We wish to establish the holomorphicity of (
58
) around some point
m_{0}\in M
.
We assume without loss of generality that
M\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, and that
I\subset S^{1}
.
As a first step, we wish to extend
\alpha:M\times I\to A
to a pointwise-holomorphic map
\hat{\alpha}:M\times S^{1}\to A
with the property that each restriction
\hat{\alpha}|_{\{m\}\times S^{1}}:S^{1}\to A
is an embedding with winding number
1
(it represents the standard generator of
\pi_{1}(A)
).
For that, first pick
\hat{\alpha}|_{\{m_{0}\}\times S^{1}}
extending
\alpha|_{\{m_{0}\}\times S^{1}}
.
For every point
q\in I
, the map
\hat{\alpha}|_{M\times\{q\}}
is determined by the coefficients of its Taylor series (which are smooth functions on
I
). We extend these coefficients arbitrarily to smooth functions on
S^{1}
, and declare them to be the Taylor coefficient of some map
M\times S^{1}\to A
. Provided these smooth extensions have their sup-norm controlled by their values at
\partial I
(e.g. they are bounded by twice those values), and that they vanish identically on the subset of
S^{1}
which maps to
\partial A
, the corresponding Taylor series will have positive radius of convergence and will determine, locally, a family of embeddings
S^{1}\to A
parametrised by the points of
M
.

This family of embeddings
\hat{\alpha}|_{\{m\}\times S^{1}}:S^{1}\to A
provides a family of decompositions
A=B(m)\cup C(m)
in
\Ann
.
By
[
HT24
, Prop. 5.11]
, we may (locally) choose
z
-lifts
\underline{B}(m)
,
\underline{C}(m)\in\tAnn_{c}
, which depend holomorphically on
m
.
These
z
-lifts may furthermore be chosen so that
\underline{A}=\underline{B}(m)\underline{C}(m)
.
The corresponding operators
\underline{B}(m)
and
\underline{C}(m)
in
{\mathcal{B}}(K)
depend holomorphically on
m
by
[
HT25
, Thm. 6.4]
.
It follows that
\underline{A}[x(m)]=\underline{B}(m)x\underline{C}(m)
also depends holomorphically on
m
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
D
be a disc.
Let
M
be a finite dimensional complex manifold, and let
\alpha_{i}:M\times I_{i}\to\mathring{D}
be smooth maps which are pointwise holomorphic in the sense that
\forall q\in I_{i}

\alpha_{i}|_{M\times\{q\}}:M\to\mathring{D}

is holomorphic. Let us further assume that for every point
m\in M
, the maps
\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}}
are embeddings with disjoint images.
For
m\in M
, let
I_{i}(m)
and
x_{i}(m)
be as in Lemma
5.8
.
Then the map

M\to H_{0}(D):m\mapsto|x_{1}(m)x_{2}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)\rangle_{D}

is holomorphic.

Proof.

The result being local in
M
, we may assume without loss of generality that there exists a disc
D_{in}\subset D
which is disjoint from the images of all the
\alpha_{i}
.
Let
A:=D\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
.
The result then follows from Lemma
5.8
, as

|x_{1}(m)x_{2}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)\rangle_{D}=A[x_{1}(m)x_{2}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]\Omega_{D_{in}}.\qed

The same arguments used to establish holomorphicity of worm insertions can also be used to show continuity all the way up to the boundary:

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
M
be a topological space.

i)

Let
D
be a disc, and let
\alpha_{i}:M\times I_{i}\to D
be a continuous family of embeddings of
I_{i}
into
D
(the corresponding map
M\to C^{\infty}(I_{i},D)
is continuous). Let us further assume that for every point
m\in M
, the maps
\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}}
have disjoint images.
For
m\in M
, let
I_{i}(m):=\alpha_{i}(\{m\}\times I_{i})
and

x_{i}(m):=(\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}})_{*}(x_{i})\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i}(m))
.
Then the map

M\to H_{0}(D):m\mapsto|x_{1}(m)x_{2}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)\rangle_{D}

(59)

is continuous.

ii)

Let
A:=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus, and let
\alpha_{i}:M\times I_{i}\to A
be a continuous family of smooth embeddings of
I_{i}
into
A
.
Assume that for every point
m\in M
, the the maps
\alpha_{i}|_{\{m\}\times I_{i}}
are embeddings with disjoint images, and
let
I_{i}(m)
and
x_{i}(m)
be as above.
Then the map

M\to{\mathcal{B}}\big(H_{0}(D_{in}),H_{0}(D_{out})\big):m\mapsto A[x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)]

is continuous for the strong operator topology on
{\mathcal{B}}(H_{0}(D_{in}),H_{0}(D_{out}))
.

iii)

Similarly, given a
z
-lift
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
of
A
, and a representation
K
of the conformal net

M\to{\mathcal{B}}(K):m\mapsto\underline{A}[a_{1}(m)\ldots a_{n}(m)]

is continuous for the strong operator topology on
{\mathcal{B}}(K)
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to those of Lemma
5.8
and Corollary
5.9
,
with

[
HT24
, Prop. 5.5]

in place of
[
HT24
, Prop. 5.11]
,
and the continuity statement in
[
HT25
, Thm. 6.4]

in place of the holomorphicity statement in
[
HT25
, Thm. 6.4]
.
∎

5.3
Unoriented worms

Recall that
{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the standard unit disc. Let
I_{-}\subset\partial{\mathbb{D}}
be the lower semi-circle, let
I_{+}:=I_{-}^{\prime}\subset\partial{\mathbb{D}}
be the upper semi-circle (so that
\partial I_{-}=\partial I_{+}=\{\pm 1\}
),
and
let
\delta:i\mathbb{R}\to\Aut({\mathbb{D}})
be the unique action which fixes
\{\pm 1\}
, normalised so that the derivative at
1
of
\delta_{it}
is
e^{t}
.
Recall from Section
4.4
that the Bisognano-Wichmann theorem for conformal nets provides a canonical identification of
H_{0}=H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
with the
L^{2}
-space of the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
. Under that identification, the rescaled modular flow
\Delta^{it/2\pi}:L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})\to L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
agrees with the induced action of
\delta_{it}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
on
H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
.

The homomorphism
i{\mathbb{R}}\to\Mob:it\mapsto\delta_{it}
can be analytically continued to a homomorphism
{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Aut}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}):z\mapsto\delta_{z}
,
which again fixes the points
\pm 1
.
For example, the map
\delta_{\pi}
exchanges
I_{-}
and
I_{+}
:

\delta_{\pi}\,:\hskip-5.69046pt\hbox to228.02pt{\vbox to105.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 114.01103pt\lower-52.83777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -78.74 0 M -55.45 -64 C -42.31 -59.22 -30.97 -50.52 -23.27 -39.52}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.5736}{0.81914}{-0.81914}{0.5736}{-16.69933pt}{-28.3971pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.9}{0.0}{0.0}{0.9}{31.49594pt}{-3.4527pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -44.32 -0.41 L -55.68 -0.41 L -55.68 -3.53 L -44.32 -3.53 Z M -55.68 -3.53}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.9}{0.0}{0.0}{0.9}{-41.14127pt}{-3.45268pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

As explained in Section
4.4
, the modular conjugation
J:L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})\to L^{2}{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
corresponds to an operator
\Theta:H_{0}\to H_{0}
which satisfies
\Theta({\mathcal{A}}(I))\Theta={\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta I)
, where
\vartheta(z)=\bar{z}
. Let us define

\theta:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta(I)),\qquad\theta(x):=\Theta x\Theta.

As explained in
[
BDH15
, §2A]
,
the map
\theta
is, up to a
\ast
, a special case of the map
{\mathcal{A}}(f):{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(J)

associated to an orientation reversing diffeomorphism
f:I\to J
:

\theta:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta I)\qquad\theta(x)={\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta)(x^{*}).

If
I
is an interval, let us write
\overline{I}
for that same interval equipped with the opposite orientation, and let
j_{I}:I\to\overline{I}
denote the identity map viewed as an orientation reversing map from
I
to
\overline{I}
.
For
y\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
, let us then write
j(y):={\mathcal{A}}(j_{I})(y)\in{\mathcal{A}}(\overline{I})
for its image under the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear anti-homomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}(j_{I}):{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(\overline{I})
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
y\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
and
j(y)\in{\mathcal{A}}(\overline{I})
be as above.

i)

Let
D
be a disc, let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y
be worms in
D
, and let us assume that the support interval
I
of
y
is contained in the interior of
D
. Then:

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y\rangle_{D}=|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)\rangle_{D}.

(60)

ii)

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus, let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be worms in
A
, and let
y
be a worm with support interval
I\subset\mathring{A}
.
Then:

A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]=A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)].

(61)

iii)

If
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is a
z
-lift of
A
, and
y
is as above, then, in any representation
K
of the conformal net, we have:

\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]=\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)].

(62)

Proof.

i)

Since both sides of (
60
) depend continuously on the support intervals of the worms (Lemma
5.10
), we may assume without loss of generality that each of the worms
x_{i}
and
y
are supported in the interior of our disc
D
, and that their support intervals are analytically embedded.
In this case, we may write
D=A\cup D^{\prime}
where
A
is an annulus containing the worms
x_{j}
, and
D^{\prime}
is a disc containing the support interval of
y
in its interior.
We then have:

|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y\rangle_{D}=A[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]|y\rangle_{D^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)\rangle_{D}=B[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}]|j(y)\rangle_{D^{\prime}}.

Thus, by replacing
D
by
D^{\prime}
, it is enough to treat the case of a single insertion (i.e.
n=0
).
By choosing
D^{\prime}
small enough, we may furthermore assume that there exists a holomorphic embedding
f:D^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{D}}
mapping the support interval
I
of
y
to some interval on the real axis.
Applying
Y_{{\mathbb{D}}\setminus f(D^{\prime})}
to the vectors
|y\rangle_{D^{\prime}}
and
|j(y)\rangle_{D^{\prime}}
and using the injectivity of
Y_{{\mathbb{D}}\setminus f(D^{\prime})}
(Lemmas
4.15
and
4.21
),
we are further reduced to the case
D={\mathbb{D}}
, with a single worm insertion whose support interval
I
lies on the real axis.

Let
I_{-}\subset\partial{\mathbb{D}}
be the lower semicircle.
By the Bisognano-Wichman theorem,
for any element
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
, the map
i{\mathbb{R}}\to H_{0}
given by
it\mapsto\Delta^{it/2\pi}x\Omega

admits an extension

F:\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,0\leq\Re\mathrm{e}(z)\leq\pi\}\to H_{0}

which is continuous on the closed strip and holomorphic in the open strip.
It satisfies

F(it)=\delta_{it}(x)\Omega\,\,\,\quad\text{and}\,\,\,\quad F(\pi+it)=\Theta\delta_{it}(x^{*})\Omega=\theta(\delta_{it}(x^{*}))\Omega=\delta_{it}(\theta(x^{*}))\Omega

for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(where
\delta_{it}(x)
denotes the image of
x
under the map
{\mathcal{A}}(\delta_{it}):{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})\to{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
).

Let us now set
x:=\delta_{-\pi/2}(y)\in{\mathcal{A}}(\delta_{-\pi/2}(I))\subset{\mathcal{A}}(I_{-})
, and
consider the map

F_{1}:\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,0\leq\Re\mathrm{e}(z)<\pi\}\to H_{0}

given by

F_{1}(z)=|\delta_{z}(x)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}
.
Here,
\delta_{z}(x)
denotes the image of the element
x
under the map
{\mathcal{A}}(\delta_{z}):{\mathcal{A}}(\delta_{-\pi/2}(I))\to{\mathcal{A}}(\delta_{-\pi/2+z}(I))
.
Importantly, note that when
0<\Re\mathrm{e}(z)<\pi
we have
\delta_{-\pi/2+z}(I)\subset\mathring{{\mathbb{D}}}
, hence
|\delta_{z}(x)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}
is well defined.
The map
F_{1}
is continuous (Lemma
5.10
), and holomorphic in the interior of the strip (Corollary
5.9
).
By analytic continuation, since
F
and
F_{1}
agree on
i{\mathbb{R}}
, they also agree in the interior of the strip.

Next, consider the map

F_{2}:\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,0<\Re\mathrm{e}(z)\leq\pi\}\to H_{0}

given by
F_{2}(z)=|\delta_{z}(j(x))\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=|\delta_{z-\pi}(\theta(x^{*}))\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

The map
F_{2}
is again continuous and holomorphic in the interior.
The functions
F_{2}
and
F
agree on
\pi+i{\mathbb{R}}
,
therefore agree on the interior of the strip.
It follows that
F_{1}(z)=F_{2}(z)
for
0<\Re\mathrm{e}(z)<\pi
.
Specialising to
z=\pi/2
, we get

|y\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=F_{1}(\pi/2)=F_{2}(\pi/2)=|j(y)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}
, as desired.

ii)
Once again, both sides of (
62
) depend continuously on the support intervals of the worm, and we may assume without loss of generality that the worms are supported in the interior of
A
.
Write
A=B\cup C
, where
B
is a thin annulus containing the support intervals of all the worms.
We then have

B[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]\Omega_{D_{in}}=|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y\rangle_{B\cup D_{in}}=|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)\rangle_{B\cup D_{in}}=B[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)]\Omega_{D_{in}},

where the middle equality follows from part
i)
.
As
B
is thin, it follows from the Reeh-Schlieder theorem that

B[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]=B[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)]
.
The desired statement follows.

iii)
We may again assume that the worms are located in the interior of
A
.
Write
\underline{A}
as a product
\underline{A}=\underline{B}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{C}
of
z
-lifted annuli, with the property that
\underline{B}\in\tAnn_{c}(I_{0})
for some interval
I_{0}
, and such that
B
contains the support intervals of all of the worms.
We then have

\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]=\underline{B}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y]\underline{C}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{A}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)]=\underline{B}[x_{1}\ldots x_{n}j(y)]\underline{C},

and we are thus reduced to establishing the desired identity with
\underline{B}
in place of
\underline{A}
.
By Lemma
5.6
,
it suffices to consider when
K=H_{0}
is the vacuum sector, in which case the desired identity follows from part
ii)
by Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Recall that
\vartheta:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}:z\mapsto\overline{z}
denotes complex conjugation, and that
\Theta:H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
is the corresponding antiunitary operator. For an interval
I\subset{\mathbb{D}}
, let us write
\theta:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to{\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta(I))

for the map
\theta(x)={\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta)(x^{*})
,
where
\vartheta(I)
is oriented in such a way that
\vartheta:I\to\vartheta(I)
is orientation reversing.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
be disjoint intervals, and let
x_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})
.
Then

\Theta(|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}})=|\theta(x_{1})\ldots\theta(x_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

(63)

Proof.

By continuity (Lemma
5.10
), we may assume without loss of generality that the support intervals
I_{j}
are all in
\mathring{\mathbb{D}}
.
Pick a disc
D\subset{\mathbb{D}}
such that the intervals
I_{j}
are contained in
\partial D
, and let
A={\mathbb{D}}\setminus\mathring{D}
. By choosing a single interval
I\subset\partial D
containing every
I_{j}
and letting
x=x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I)
, we are reduced to proving equation (
63
) in the case
n=1
:

\Theta(|x\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}})=|\theta(x)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

Choose a Riemann map
\varphi:D\to{\mathbb{D}}
.
Parametrize the incoming boundary of
A
by
\varphi^{-1}
, and equip the outgoing boundary
S^{1}=\partial{\mathbb{D}}
with the identity parametrization, so that
A\in\Univ
.
Letting
\overline{\varphi}:=\vartheta\circ\varphi\circ\vartheta
,
we then have:

\displaystyle\Theta|x\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=\Theta A|\varphi(x)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=\Theta A\varphi(x)\Omega

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\Theta\varphi(x)\Omega

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\theta(\varphi(x))\Omega=\overline{A}\overline{\varphi}(\theta(x))\Omega=\overline{A}|\overline{\varphi}(\theta(x))\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}=|\theta(x)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}},

where the third equality holds by (
37
) and (
21
) (and we’ve written
\overline{A}
for the action (
21
) of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
on
A\in\Univ
).
∎

Recall that if

f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
is an embedding which is either holomorphic or anti-holomorphic, then the pushforward
f_{*}:H_{0}(D_{1})\to H_{0}(D_{2})
was defined in (
38
).

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be discs, let
x_{i}
be worms in
D_{1}
, and let
f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
be an embedding that is either holomorphic or anti-holomorphic.
Then

f_{*}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D_{1}}=|{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n})\rangle_{D_{2}}

(64)

when
f
is holomorphic, and

f_{*}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D_{1}}=|{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1}^{*})\ldots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{n}^{*})\rangle_{D_{2}}

(65)

when
f
is anti-holomorphic (in the latter case, if
I_{i}
is the support interval of
x_{i}
, then the support interval
f(I_{i})
of
{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{i}^{*})
is oriented in such a way that the map
f:I_{i}\to f(I_{i})
is orientation reversing).

Proof.

When
f
is holomorphic, equation (
64
) is a special case of (
48
).

For
f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
anti-holomorphic,
write it as a composite

D_{1}\to{\mathbb{D}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartheta}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{D}}\to D_{2}

where the first and third maps are holomorphic, and apply
Lemma
5.12
(which is the special case of (
65
) for
f=\vartheta
) to the middle map.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
D
be a disc, let
D^{\dagger}
be its complex conjugate, and the
i:D\to D^{\dagger}
be the anti-holomorphic identity map. If the support intervals of the worms are contained in
\mathring{D}
, then we have

i_{*}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}=|x_{1}^{*}\ldots x_{n}^{*}\rangle_{D^{\dagger}}=|j(x_{1}^{*})\ldots j(x_{n}^{*})\rangle_{D^{\dagger}},

where
j
is as in Lemma
5.11
.

If the support intervals of the worms meet
\partial D
, then we still have

i_{*}|x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\rangle_{D}=|j(x_{1}^{*})\ldots j(x_{n}^{*})\rangle_{D^{\dagger}},

but the expression
|x_{1}^{*}\ldots x_{n}^{*}\rangle_{D^{\dagger}}
is no longer defined,
as the support intervals of the worms
x_{i}^{*}
fail the orientation requirements from Definition
5.1
.

6
Point insertions

6.1
Construction of point insertions

In this section, we establish a state-field correspondence between finite energy vectors
v\in H_{0}^{f.e.}
in the vacuum sector, and point-localised fields (
local operators
in the physics terminology).
Our main technical lemma establishes that every point insertion can be written as a linear combination of two worms:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
v\in H_{0}=H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
be a finite energy vector, and let
I_{1},I_{2}\subset S^{1}
be intervals whose interiors cover
S^{1}
. Then there exist
x_{1}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1})
and
x_{2}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2})
such that

v=x_{1}\Omega+x_{2}\Omega.

(66)

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
v
is homogeneous.
Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the conformal dimension of
v
, and let
p_{n}:H_{0}\to H_{0}(n)
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace of vectors of conformal dimension
n
.
The projection
p_{n}
is given by the formula

p_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}e^{-in\theta}R_{\theta}\,d\theta,

where
R_{\theta}=e^{i\theta L_{0}}
is the operator of anticlockwise rotation by
\theta
.
Let
\delta
be the length of the smallest connected component of
I_{1}\cap I_{2}
, and
let
I_{j}^{-}
be the result of shortening
I_{j}
by
\delta/3
on either side.

Let
J
be an interval of length
\delta/3
centered around
1
.
The map
{\mathcal{A}}(J)\to H_{0}:x\mapsto x\Omega
has dense image by the Reeh-Schlieder theorem.
Therefore
{\mathcal{A}}(J)\to H_{0}(n):x\mapsto p_{n}x\Omega
also has dense image.
As
H_{0}(n)
is finite-dimensional, that last map is therefore surjective.
Pick
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(J)
such that
p_{n}(x\Omega)=v
, and pick a partition of unity
\{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\}
subordinate to the cover
\{I_{1}^{-},I_{2}^{-}\}
.
Then

\displaystyle v=p_{n}(x\Omega)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}e^{-in\theta}R_{\theta}\,x\,d\theta\right)\Omega

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}e^{-in\theta}r_{\theta}(x)\,d\theta\right)\Omega

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I_{1}^{-}}\varphi_{1}(e^{i\theta})e^{-in\theta}r_{\theta}(x)\,d\theta\right)\Omega+\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I_{2}^{-}}\varphi_{2}(e^{i\theta})e^{-in\theta}r_{\theta}(x)\,d\theta\right)\Omega,

where
r_{\theta}(x)=R_{\theta}xR_{-\theta}
.
We finish by noting that since
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(J)
, we have
r_{\theta}(x)\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})
for every
\theta\in I_{j}^{-}
.
Thus setting

x_{j}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I_{j}^{-}}\varphi_{j}(e^{i\theta})e^{-in\theta}r_{\theta}(x)\,d\theta\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{j})

gives the desired decomposition (
66
).
∎

Given a point
z
in some Riemann surface
\Sigma
(in our applications,
\Sigma
will either be a disc or
{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}
), one can form the inverse limit (i.e. intersection)

H_{0}(z):=\underleftarrow{\lim}_{\mathring{D}\ni z}H_{0}(D)

of all the vacuum sectors
H_{0}(D)
, were the inverse limit runs over all discs
D\subset\Sigma
containing
z
in their interior.
Here, given two discs
D_{1}\subset D_{2}
containing
z
, the structure map in the above inverse limit is given by

Y_{A}:H_{0}(D_{1})\to H_{0}(D_{2}),

where
A
is the annulus
D_{2}\setminus\mathring{D}_{1}
.

The space
H_{0}(z)
admits an action by the group
G_{z}
of germs (defined on a neighbourhood of
z
) of invertible maps
\Sigma\to\Sigma
fixing the point
z
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
z
be a point in some Riemann surface.
The space
V(z)
of finite energy vectors at
z
is the subspace
17
17

17

We will show later, in Lemma
6.4
, that the space
V(z)
is the same as the one considered in
[
FBZ04
, Ch. 6]
, in the context of vertex algebras.

V(z):=\big\{v\in H_{0}(z)\,\big|\,\spann\{gv:g\in G_{z}\}\text{ is finite dimensional}\big\}.

(67)

If the Riemann surface is
{\mathbb{C}}
(or
{\mathbb{D}}
) and the point
z
is the origin
0\in{\mathbb{C}}
, then we denote
V(z)
simply by
V
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The space
V\subset H_{0}
is the algebraic direct sum of all the eigenspaces of
L_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the group of germs of invertible maps
{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
fixing the origin.
Consider the
1
-parameter subgroup
S^{1}\subset G
given by rotations.
If a vector
v\in H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
is not in the algebraic direct sum of the
L_{0}
-eigenspaces,
then
\spann\{gv:g\in S^{1}\}
is already infinite dimensional, so
v\not\in V
.

Conversely, if
L_{0}v=Nv
for some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we will show that
for any
g\in G
, we have
gv\in H_{0}^{\leq N}:=\bigoplus_{n\leq N}H_{0}(n)
,
and hence the span of all such vectors
gv
is finite dimensional.
Let
g\in G
be a group element.
By writing
g
as
g^{\prime}\exp(qL_{0})
for some scalar
q
and some
g^{\prime}\in\Univ_{0}
fixing the origin, we are reduced to the case
g\in\Univ_{0}
.

If
g\in\Univ_{0}
, then
the action of
g
on
V
extends to a map
H_{0}\to H_{0}
.
By
[
HT24
, Prop. 4.22]
and the fact that
g
fixes
0
, we may write
g
as a time-ordered exponential

g=\prod_{1\geq\tau\geq 0}\Exp(X_{\tau}d\tau)

where each

X_{\tau}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{m}(\tau)L_{m}

is a holomorphic vector field on
{\mathbb{D}}
that vanishes at the origin, and whose restriction to
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
is an inward pointing vector field.
The operators
L_{m}
for
m\geq 0
preserve
H_{0}^{\leq N}
. Therefore so does
g
.
∎

By the arguments in the above proof, the action of
G
(defined above) on
V
extends to the group
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
. Indeed, on each filtered piece
H_{0}^{\leq N}
, the action of
G
descends to its finite dimensional quotient
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[t]/t^{N+1})
, and the group
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
is the inverse limit of those finite dimensional quotients.

Starting from the action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
on
V
,
an alternative definition of
V(z)
goes as follows. Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface, let
z\in\Sigma
be a point, and
let
{\mathcal{O}}_{z}(\Sigma)
be the formal completion of the ring of holomorphic functions on
\Sigma
at the point
z
. We can then define

V(z)=V\times_{\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])}\mathrm{Isom}({\mathcal{O}}_{z}(\Sigma),{\mathbb{C}}[[t]]),

(68)

where we have written
\mathrm{Isom}({\mathcal{O}}_{z}(\Sigma),{\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
for the set of
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra isomorphisms between
{\mathcal{O}}_{z}(\Sigma)
and
{\mathbb{C}}[[t]]
.
This construction is functorial in
z
in the sense that whenever a holomorphic map
f:\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
sends
z_{1}
to
z_{2}
and the map is a local isomorphism around these points, then there is an induced map
f_{*}:V(z_{1})\to V(z_{2})
. When
z_{1}=z_{2}=0\in{\mathbb{C}}
, this recovers with the original action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
on
V
.
In particular, given a local coordinate at a point
z\in\Sigma
(i.e. a holomorphic map
w\mapsto f(w)
defined for
w
in some neighbourhood of
0\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and satisfying
f(0)=z
), we get an isomorphism
V\to V(z)
.
We denote this isomorphism by
v\mapsto v(z;f)
.
When the choice of local coordinate is clear from context, we write this isomorphism simply as
v\mapsto v(z)
.
Let us also write
v\mapsto v^{\natural}:V(z)\to V
for the inverse of that isomorphism (again assuming that
f
is clear from context).

Lemma 6.4
.

The two constructions (
67
) and (
68
) of
V(z)
are canonically isomorphic.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the connected groupoid whose objects are pairs
(\Sigma,z)
with
z\in\Sigma
,

and whose morphisms
(\Sigma_{1},z_{1})\to(\Sigma_{2},z_{2})
are germs of local isomorphisms
\Sigma_{1}\supset U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}\Sigma_{2}
satisfying
f(z_{1})=z_{2}
.

Both (
67
) and (
68
) are functorial in
z
in the sense that a local isomorphisms
f:\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
induces a map
f_{*}:V(z)\to V(f(z))
. In other words, both constructions are functors from
{\mathcal{G}}
to the category of vector spaces.
The two constructions visibly agree on the object
({\mathbb{C}},0)\in{\mathcal{G}}
, and on
G=\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}({\mathbb{C}},0)
.
We finish the proof by invoking the general fact that two functors
F,G:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
which are naturally equivalent when restricted to a skeleton of
{\mathcal{C}}
are in fact already naturally equivalent.
∎

Definition 6.5
.

Given a vector space
V
equipped with an action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
,
we let
\Ann^{V}
denote the semigroup whose elements consists of triples
(A,\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\},\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\})
where
A
is an annulus,
z_{i}\in\mathring{A}
are an unordered collection of points in the interior of
A
, and
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
(where
V(z_{i})
is defined as in (
68
)).
We will typically denote such a triple
A[v_{1}\ldots v_{n}]
, or alternatively
A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]
when we wish to make the insertion points explicit.

The central extensions
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
and
\Ann_{c}^{V}
are defined in the same way, using an element
\underline{A}
of
\tAnn_{c}
or
\Ann_{c}
instead of
A\in\Ann
.

When
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
, and the points
z_{i}
are equipped with local coordinates
f_{i}
, we can then form
A[v_{1}\ldots v_{n}]\in\Ann^{V}
by implicitly replacing the
v_{i}
by their images
v_{i}(z_{i};f_{i})
under the isomorphism
V\cong V(z_{i})
.
For example,
given an annulus
A
equipped with an embedding
A\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, along with points
z_{i}\in\mathring{A}
and vectors
v_{i}\in V
, we will typically identify the vector spaces
V(z_{i})
with
V
using the standard local coordinates
w\mapsto w+z_{i}
.

If
D
is a disc and
v\in V(z)
for some point
z\in\mathring{D}
, let us write
|v\rangle_{D}
for the image of
v
in
H_{0}(D)
.
By definition, these satisfy

Y_{D_{2}\setminus\mathring{D}_{1}}(|v\rangle_{D_{1}})=|v\rangle_{D_{2}}

(69)

whenever
D_{1}\subset D_{2}
.

The following is an immediate corollary of Lemma
6.1
:

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
D
be a disc, let
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\partial D
be intervals whose interiors cover
\partial D
, and let
z\in\mathring{D}
be a point in the interior of the disc.
Then for every vector
v\in V(z)
there exist
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
such that
|v\rangle_{D}=x_{1}\Omega_{D}+x_{2}\Omega_{D}
.

For every configuration of
n
distinct points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{D}
, and vectors
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
, we will now describe a vector

|v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D).

(70)

Pick disjoint discs
D_{i}\subset D
containing the points
z_{i}
in their respective interiors.
Using Corollary
6.6
, pick intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
and algebra elements
x_{1,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1,i})
and
x_{2,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2,i})
such that

|v_{i}\rangle_{D_{i}}=x_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}+x_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}

in
H_{0}(D_{i})
.
We then define
|v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D)
to be the sum of the following
2^{n}
terms:

\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{D}.

(71)

We will show below that this is independent of the choices of discs
D_{i}
, intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}
, and algebra elements
x_{\epsilon,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{\epsilon,i})
).

Lemma 6.7
.

The vector (
71
) is independent of the choices of discs
D_{i}
, intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, and algebra elements
x_{1,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1,i})
and
x_{2,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2,i})
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that (
71
) remains unchanged when one of the discs
D_{i}
gets replaced by a smaller disc
D^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq D_{i}
, and the two worms
x_{1,i}
and
x_{2,i}
supported on
\partial D_{i}
get replaced by worms
x^{\prime}_{1,i}
and
x^{\prime}_{2,i}
supported on
\partial D^{\prime}_{i}
.
Without loss of generality we may take
i=1
.
Let
A=D\setminus\mathring{D}_{1}
,
A^{\prime}=D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime}_{1}
, and
A^{\prime\prime}=D_{1}\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime}_{1}
.
By
(
48
)
and (
69
),
we then have:

\displaystyle\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{D}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n-1}}A[x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}]\left(|x_{1,1}\rangle_{D_{1}}+|x_{2,1}\rangle_{D_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n-1}}A[x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}](|v_{1}\rangle_{D_{1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n-1}}A[x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}]A^{\prime\prime}(|v_{1}\rangle_{D^{\prime}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n-1}}A^{\prime}[x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}]\left(|x^{\prime}_{1,1}\rangle_{D^{\prime}_{1}}+|x^{\prime}_{2,1}\rangle_{D^{\prime}_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|x^{\prime}_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{D}.

∎

As in (
71
), given an annulus
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
, points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{A}
, and vectors
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
, we can define an operator

A[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
.
Pick disjoint discs
D_{i}\subset\mathring{A}
containing the points
z_{i}
in their interiors,
intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
and algebra elements
x_{\epsilon,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{\epsilon,i})

satisfying

|v_{i}\rangle_{D_{i}}=x_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}+x_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}
, and set

A[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]:=\!\!\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}\!A[x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}]:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out}).

(72)

Finally, given a
z
-lifted annulus
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
with underlying annulus
A
, points
z_{i}\in\mathring{A}
and
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
as above, and a representation
K
of the conformal net, we can form

\underline{A}[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]:=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}\underline{A}[x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}]\,:\,K\,\to\,K.

(73)

As before, we will need to show that (
72
) and (
73
) are independent of the choices of discs
D_{i}
, and algebra elements
x_{\epsilon,i}
.
This will be proven further down in Lemma
6.9
.

We have the following immediate consequence of Lemma
5.6
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
be localised in some interval
I
.
Then the operator

\underline{A}[v_{1}\ldots v_{n}]:H_{0}\to H_{0}

(74)

defined in (
73
) lies in
{\mathcal{A}}(I)\subset B(H_{0})
.
Moreover, if
(K,\rho)
is a representation of the conformal net then

\underline{A}[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]:K\to K

is the image of (
74
)
under the homomorphism

\rho_{I}:{\mathcal{A}}(I)\to B(K)
.
\square

Lemma 6.9
.

The operators (
72
) and (
73
) are
independent of the choices of discs
D_{i}\subset A
, intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, and algebra elements
x_{1,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1,i})
and
x_{2,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2,i})
.

Proof.

We treat both cases in parallel.
Let
A
be an annulus which is either of the form
D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
, or an element of
\tAnn_{c}
.
By writing
A
as a composite
A=BC
, where
B
is thin/localised and contains all the insertion points, we are reduced to proving the statements for thin/localised annuli.

If
\underline{A}
in (
73
) is localised, then by Lemma
6.8
the choice of representation is immaterial. So it’s enough to prove the statement when
K=H_{0}
; we henceforth assume that to be the case.
By Lemma
5.3
,
the independence of (
73
) from all the choices is then a consequence of the corresponding independence statement for (
72
).
We are thus reduced to proving that (
72
) is independent of the choices of discs
D_{i}
, intervals
I_{\epsilon,i}
, and algebra elements
x_{\epsilon,i}
, when
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is a thin annulus.

Let
I
be an interval in the thin part of
\partial A
.
By Lemma
6.7
, the expression

A[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]\Omega_{D_{in}}=|v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\rangle_{D_{out}}.

is independent of all choices.
As a consequence of the Reeh-Schlieder theorem, the vacuum vector
\Omega_{D_{in}}
is separating among operators
H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
that commute with
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
.
The operator
A[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]
commutes with the actions of
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
by Lemma
5.5
. It follows that
A[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]
is also independent of all the choices.
∎

By construction, if
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is an annulus, we have

A[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]|u_{1}u_{2}\ldots u_{m}\rangle_{D_{in}}=|v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}u_{1}u_{2}\ldots u_{m}\rangle_{D_{out}}.

(75)

For
\underline{A}_{1},\underline{A}_{2}\in\tAnn_{c}
, and
K
a representation of the conformal net, we also have

\underline{A}_{1}[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]\underline{A}_{2}[u_{1}u_{2}\ldots u_{m}]=\underline{A}_{1}\underline{A}_{2}[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}u_{1}\ldots u_{m}].

(76)

This gives an action on
K
of the semigroup
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
(defined in Definition
6.5
). And when
K=H_{0}
, that action descends to
\Ann_{c}^{V}
.

Note that the relationship between the operators associated to parametrised and to unparametrised annuli established in Lemmas
4.21
and
5.3
extends verbatim to the case of point insertions:

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus with boundary parametrizations
\varphi_{in/out}:S^{1}\to\partial D_{in/out}
, and let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
be a
z
-lift.
Pick unitaries
U(\varphi_{in/out}):H_{0}\to H_{0}(D_{in/out})
implementing
\varphi_{in/out}
, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
satisfying

\underline{A}=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}AU(\varphi_{in})
.
Then the same relation holds in the presence of point insertions:

\underline{A}[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]=z\cdot U(\varphi_{out})^{*}A[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]U(\varphi_{in}).

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
5.3
and the definition of
\underline{A}[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]
.
∎

The following is immediate from Lemma
5.5
:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be a thin annulus, and let
I
be in the thin part of
A
.
Then

A[v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{n}]:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})

is an
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
-module map.

\square

We finish this section with an analog of Lemma
5.4
:

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
be a univalent map, and let
\underline{A}_{f}\in\Univ\subset\Ann_{c}
be the corresponding lifted annulus.
Let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{{\mathbb{D}}}
and
w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\in\mathring{A}_{f}
be points satisfying
z_{i}\neq z_{j}
and
w_{i}\neq w_{j}
when
i\neq j
, and let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in V
be vectors.
Then

\displaystyle\underline{A}_{f}[u_{1}(w_{1})

\displaystyle\ldots u_{m}(w_{m})]\big(|v_{1}(z_{1};f_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n};f_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\big)

\displaystyle=|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{m}(w_{m})v_{1}(f(z_{1});f\circ f_{1})\ldots v_{n}(f(z_{n});f\circ f_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

(where we use the standard local coordinates around the points
w_{i}
).

Proof.

Pick discs
D_{i}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
around
z_{i}
, intervals
I_{\epsilon,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
, and elements
x_{\epsilon,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{\epsilon,i})
satisfying

|v_{i}(z;f_{i})\rangle_{D_{i}}=x_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}+x_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}.

Similarly, pick discs
D_{i}^{\prime}\subset A_{f}
, intervals
J_{\epsilon,i}\subset\partial D_{i}^{\prime}
, and elements
y_{\epsilon,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(J_{\epsilon,i})
such that

|u_{i}(w_{i})\rangle_{D_{i}^{\prime}}=y_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}^{\prime}}+y_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}^{\prime}}.

Writing
U_{i}:H_{0}(D_{i})\to H_{0}(f(D_{i}))
for the unitary induced by
f:D_{i}\to f(D_{i})
, we then have

\hskip-5.69054pt|v_{i}(f(z);f\circ f_{i})\rangle_{f(D_{i})}=U_{i}|v_{i}(z;f_{i})\rangle_{D_{i}}=U_{i}(x_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}+x_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}})={\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{1,i})\Omega_{f(D_{i})}+{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{2,i})\Omega_{f(D_{i})}.

Hence, by Lemma
5.4
, we have:

\displaystyle\underline{A}_{f}[

\displaystyle u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{m}(w_{m})]\big(|v_{1}(z_{1};f_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n};f_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}\\
(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\in\{1,2\}^{m}\end{subarray}}\underline{A}_{f}[y_{\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},1}\ldots y_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime},m}]|x_{\epsilon_{1},1}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}\\
(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\in\{1,2\}^{m}\end{subarray}}|y_{\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},1}\ldots y_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime},m}\,{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{\epsilon_{1},1})\cdots{\mathcal{A}}(f)(x_{\epsilon_{n},n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{m}(w_{m})\,v_{1}(f(z_{1});f\circ f_{1})\ldots v_{n}(f(z_{n});f\circ f_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}.\qed

6.2
Holomorphicity of point insertions

Given a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the space

V_{\Sigma}:=\coprod_{z\in\Sigma}V(z)\to\Sigma

(77)

is a holomorphic bundle (more precisely, an inductive limit of finite dimensional holomorphic bundles).
Indeed, the bundle

J\Sigma:=\coprod_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{Isom}({\mathcal{O}}_{z}(\Sigma),{\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\to\Sigma
is a principal bundle for the group
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
, and
V_{\Sigma}=V\times_{\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])}J\Sigma
is an associated bundle
(see
[
FBZ04
, Ch. 6]
).

Given an embedding
\Sigma_{1}\hookrightarrow\Sigma_{2}
, we have a natural isomorphism between
V_{\Sigma_{1}}
and the restriction of
V_{\Sigma_{2}}
to
\Sigma_{1}
.
For example, if
\Sigma
is embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}
, then
V_{\Sigma}
is the trivial bundle
\Sigma\times V
,
and holomorphic section of
V_{\Sigma}
are the same thing as holomorphic functions
\Sigma\to V
.
When that is case, then for any vector
v\in V
we can associate the corresponding constant function on
\Sigma
; we denote the induced section of
V_{\Sigma}
by
z\mapsto v(z)
.
Note that this
v(z)
agrees with what we had previously denoted
v(z;w\mapsto w+z)
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
M
be a finite dimensional complex manifold.

i)

Let
D
be a disc,
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}:M\to\mathring{D}
be holomorphic functions,
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}:M\to V_{D}
be holomorphic lifts of those functions to
V_{D}
(defined in (
77
)).
Then

m\mapsto|v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)\rangle_{D}

is a holomorphic function from
M
to
H_{0}(D)
.

ii)

Let
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
be an annulus,
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}:M\to\mathring{A}
be holomorphic functions,
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}:M\to V_{A}
be holomorphic lifts. Then

m\mapsto A[v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)]

(78)

is a holomorphic function from
M
to the space of bounded linear maps
H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
.

iii)

If
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is a
z
-lift of
A
,
z_{i}:M\to\mathring{A}
and
v_{i}:M\to V_{A}
are as above, and
K
is a representation of the conformal net, then

m\mapsto\underline{A}[v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)]

(79)

is a holomorphic function from
M
to the space of bounded linear maps
K\to K
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case of annuli, as given a disc
D=D_{out}
we may, locally in
M
, choose a disc
D_{in}\subset D_{out}
which is disjoint from the images of the
z_{i}
and write

|v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)\rangle_{D}=A[v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)]\Omega_{D_{in}}.

It also suffices to consider the case of a single insertion, as we may decompose (again locally in
M
)

\underline{A}[v_{1}(m)\ldots v_{n}(m)]=\underline{A}_{1}[v_{1}(m)]\ldots\underline{A}_{n}[v_{n}(m)],

and similarly for the non-parametrised version.

We treat the two cases (
78
) and (
79
) in parallel.
Let
A
be an annulus.
Let
z(m)
be the location of the insertion, and let
v(m)
be the vector that is being inserted. Let us also assume without loss of generality that
A
is embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}
, so that the section
v(m)
is given by a holomorphic function
M\to V
.
The image of such a function necessarily lies in a finite dimensional subspace of
V
.
After picking a basis
\{v_{i}\}
of that subspace, we may write
v(m)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(m)v_{i}
, where
f_{i}
are scalar-valued holomorphic functions on
M
.
We are therefore reduced to the case where the vector
v(m)
is independent of
m\in M
.
Call that vector
v
.

At this stage, may assume without loss of generality that
M=\mathring{A}
. Our task is to show that
A[v(z)]

depends holomorphically on the point
z\in\mathring{A}
(where
v(z)\in V(z)
is the image of
v
under the isomorphism
V\cong V(z)
induced by the standard local coordinate).
We do this locally around some point
z_{0}\in\mathring{A}
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be such that the closed ball
B(z_{0},2\varepsilon)
is contained in
\mathring{A}
, and let us
pick intervals
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\partial B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
and elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
such that

|v(z_{0})\rangle_{B(z_{0},\varepsilon)}=x_{1}\Omega_{B(z_{0},\varepsilon)}+x_{2}\Omega_{B(z_{0},\varepsilon)}.

For every
z\in B(z,\varepsilon)
, let
I_{i}(z)\subset\partial B(z,\varepsilon)
be the image of
I_{i}
under translation by
z-z_{0}
.
Similarly, let
x_{i}(z)\in A(I_{i}(z))
be the image of
x_{i}
under the same translation.
We have
|v(z)\rangle_{B(z,\varepsilon)}=x_{1}(z)\Omega_{B(z,\varepsilon)}+x_{2}(z)\Omega_{B(z,\varepsilon)}
so that, by definition,

A[v(z)]=A[x_{1}(z)]+A[x_{2}(z)].

The operators
A[x_{1}(z)]
and
A[x_{2}(z)]
depend holomorphically on
z
by Lemma
5.8
, so
A[v(z)]
also depends holomorphically on
z
.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

There is a slightly stronger version of the above lemma (and also of Lemma
5.8
) where we also allows the annulus to vary holomorphically as a function of
m\in M
.
This stronger statement follows readily from the lemma by factoring the annuli, locally in
M
, as a product of an annulus with fixed geometry and varying point insertions, and two annuli with no point insersions and a geometry that depends on
m\in M
.

The following statement is essentially equivalent to Lemma
6.13
:

Corollary 6.15
.

If
D
is a disc embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}
, then
|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D)
depends holomorphically on the points
z_{i}\in D
, and the vectors
v_{i}\in V
.

Similarly, if
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}
is an annulus embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is a
z
-lift of
A
, then the operators

\underline{A}[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]:K\to K
and
A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]:H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
depend holomorphically on the points
z_{i}\in A
, and the vectors
v_{i}\in V
.

6.3
Covariance for orientation reversing maps

Recall that
\Theta:H_{0}\to H_{0}
is the antiunitary operator corresponding to the map
z\mapsto\overline{z}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
.
Since
\Theta
commutes with
L_{0}
(as it commutes with the real
1
-parameter semigroup generated by
L_{0}
), we have
\Theta(V)=V
.

We know from Lemma
6.1
that given a finite energy vector
v\in V\subset H_{0}
, we can find intervals
I_{1},I_{2}\subset S^{1}
and algebra elements
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{i})
such that

v=x_{1}\Omega+x_{2}\Omega=|x_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}+|x_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}.

The vector
\Theta(v)\in V
can then be expressed as

\Theta(v)=|\theta(x_{1})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}+|\theta(x_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

(80)

because
\Theta(v)=\Theta(x_{1}\Omega+x_{2}\Omega)=\Theta(x_{1}\Theta\Omega+x_{2}\Theta\Omega)=\theta(x_{1})\Omega+\theta(x_{2})\Omega
.

The following is a version of Lemma
5.12
for point-insertions:

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
and let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{{\mathbb{D}}}
be distinct points.
Then

\Theta\big(|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}\big)=|(\Theta v_{1})(\overline{z_{1}})\ldots(\Theta v_{n})(\overline{z_{n}})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

Proof.

Pick disjoint discs
D_{i}\subset D
containing the points
z_{i}
in their interiors, intervals
I_{1,i},I_{2,i}\subset\partial D_{i}
and elements
x_{1,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1,i})
,
x_{2,i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2,i})
satisfying

|v_{i}\rangle_{D_{i}}=x_{1,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}+x_{2,i}\Omega_{D_{i}}
so that,
by definition,

|v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

We then have:

\displaystyle\Theta(|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}})

\displaystyle=\Theta\big(\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|x_{\epsilon_{1},1}x_{\epsilon_{2},2}\cdots x_{\epsilon_{n},n}\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}|\theta(x_{\epsilon_{1},1})\theta(x_{\epsilon_{2},2})\cdots\theta(x_{\epsilon_{n},n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}

\displaystyle=|(\Theta v_{1})(\overline{z_{1}})\ldots(\Theta v_{n})(\overline{z_{n}})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}

where the second equality holds by Lemma
5.12
, and the third one follows from (
80
).
∎

Recall that the action of
\Vir_{\geq 0}:=\mathrm{Span}\{L_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
on
V

exponentiates to an action of the pro-algebraic group
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
. We wish to extend it to an action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
, where the generator
\vartheta
of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts by complex conjugation:

\vartheta\cdot\left(\sum a_{i}t^{i}\right)=\sum\overline{a_{i}}t^{i}.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
I_{-}\subset S^{1}
be the lower semicircle, and let
\Theta:V\to V
be the restriction to
V=H_{0}^{f.e.}
of the operator with same name defined in (
34
). Then the action of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
on
V
togehter with the involution
\Theta
assemble to an action on
V
of the semidirect product

\Aut_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])=\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2.

Proof.

We have a zigzag of inclusions

\Diff_{c}(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\Ann_{c}\hookleftarrow\Univ_{0}\hookrightarrow\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]]),

where
\Univ_{0}
is the sub-semigroup of univalent maps that fix the origin.
As in (
37
), the action of
\Ann_{c}(S^{1})
on
H_{0}
extends to an action of the semidirect product
\Ann_{c}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
The sub-semigroup of univalent maps
\Univ_{0}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
therefore also acts on
H_{0}
.

The action of
\Univ_{0}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
preserves
V
(this is evident using Definition
6.2
). Since
\Univ_{0}
generates a dense subgroup in
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
, the commutation relations between
\Theta
and
\Univ_{0}
defining the semidirect product extend to
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])
.
So
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts on
V
.
∎

Given a point
z\in D
in a disc, a holomorphic local coordinate
f
, and a vector
v\in V
, we have introduced the notation
v(z;f)
in Section
6.1

for the insertion
v_{1}(z)
, where
v_{1}\in V(z)
is the image of
v
under the isomorphism
V\to V(z)
induced by
f
. We can extend this notation to include anti-holomorphic local coordinates (i.e., anti-holomorphic maps
w\mapsto f(w)
with
f(0)=z
, defined for
w
in some neighbourhood of
0
). Once again, the map
f
induces an isomorphism
V\to V(z)
(which is now complex anti-linear), and we write
v(z;f)
for the insertion
v_{1}(z)
, where
v_{1}\in V(z)
is the image of
v
under the above isomorphism.

We can use this freedom to use anti-holomorphic local coordinates to equip the semigroups and
\Ann^{V}
and
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
(Definition
6.5
) with the structures of dagger-semigroups.
Recall that
\Ann
and
\tAnn_{c}
have been equipped with the structures of dagger-semigroups in Section
4
by declaring the dagger of an annulus
A
to be the complex conjugate annulus
A^{\dagger}
with incoming and outgoing boundaries exchanged.
If we write
\iota:A\to A^{\dagger}
for the anti-holomorphic “identity” map, then
this map induces an anti-linear isomorphism
V(z)\cong V(\iota(z))
which we shall denote
v\mapsto\bar{v}
.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
V
be a complex vector space equipped with a representation of
\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
which is linear on the identity component and anti-linear on the non-identity component.
We may then equip
\Ann^{V}
and
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
with the structure of a dagger-semigroup by
setting

A[v_{1}\ldots v_{n}]^{\dagger}\,:=\,A^{\dagger}[\bar{v}_{1}\ldots\bar{v}_{n}]

where
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
, and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{A}
.
The dagger structures on the central extensions
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
and
\Ann_{c}^{V}
are defined in the same way, by using the dagger structures on
\tAnn_{c}
and
\Ann_{c}
, respectively.

Let
0\in D_{in}\subset D_{out}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
be discs in the complex plane.
For
A=D_{out}\setminus D_{in}
with boundary parametrisations
\varphi_{in/out}
, we may identify
A^{\dagger}
with the image of
A
under the inversion map
\zeta\mapsto\bar{\zeta}^{-1}
, equipped with the induced boundary parametrisations.
In that case, given
v_{i}\in V
and
z_{i}\in\mathring{A}
, we have

A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]^{\dagger}=A^{\dagger}[v_{1}(z_{1})^{\dagger}\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})^{\dagger}]

where
v(z)^{\dagger}:=\tilde{v}(\bar{z}^{-1})
and
\tilde{v}=e^{\bar{z}L_{1}}(-\bar{z}^{-2})^{L_{0}}\Theta v
is the image of
v\in V
under the change of coordinate

\big(\zeta\mapsto(\bar{\zeta}-\bar{z})^{-1}+\bar{z}^{-1}\big)\in\Aut_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])=\Aut({\mathbb{C}}[[t]])\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2.

Note the similarity with the notation
Y(v,z)^{\dagger}
used in (
6
).

We finish this section by discussing how the representations of
\tAnn_{c}^{V}
constructed in (
73
) and (
76
) are compatible with the dagger structures:

Proposition 6.19
.

For any representation
K
of the conformal net, the operators
\underline{A}[v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}]
(constructed in (
73
), and well defined by
Lemma
6.9
)
give a
*
-representation of
\tAnn^{V}_{c}
on
K
.

Proof.

The map
\tAnn^{V}_{c}\to B(K)
is a representation by (
76
), so all that remains is to do is check that it is a
*
-representation.
That is, for
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
and
v_{i}\in V(z_{i})
, we must show that the corresponding operators

\underline{A}[v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}]:K\to K\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{A}^{\dagger}[\bar{v}_{1},\ldots,\bar{v}_{n}]:K\to K

are each other’s adjoints.
Since
\tAnn^{V}_{c}
is generated by
\tAnn_{c}
along with the annuli with a single insertion, we may assume without loss of generality that
n\leq 1
. The case of annuli with no insertions was treated in
[
HT25
]
(and restated in (
24
)), so we focus on the case
n=1
.
Namely, for
z\in\mathring{A}
and
v\in V(z)
, we must show that

\underline{A}[v]^{*}=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[\bar{v}]
.

Let
D\subset\mathring{A}
be a disc
containing the point
z
in its interior,
and let
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\partial D
and
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{1})
and
y\in{\mathcal{A}}(I_{2})
such that

|v\rangle_{D}=|x\rangle_{D}+|y\rangle_{D}
, so that
\underline{A}[v]=\underline{A}[x]+\underline{A}[y]
.

Letting
i:D\to D^{\dagger}
be the anti-holomorphic “identity” map, we have

|\bar{v}\rangle_{D^{\dagger}}=i_{*}(|v\rangle_{D})=i_{*}(|x\rangle_{D}+|y\rangle_{D})=|j(x^{*})\rangle_{D^{\dagger}}+|j(y^{*})\rangle_{D^{\dagger}}

(81)

(where
i_{*}
is as in (
38
)),
where the first equality holds by the definition of
\bar{v}
, and the
last equality holds by Corollary
5.14
.
It follows that

\underline{A}[v]^{*}=\underline{A}[x]^{*}+\underline{A}[y]^{*}=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[x^{*}]+\underline{A}^{\dagger}[y^{*}]=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[j(x^{*})]+\underline{A}^{\dagger}[j(y^{*})]=\underline{A}^{\dagger}[\bar{v}]

where the second equality holds by Lemma
5.7
, the third equality holds by Lemma
5.11
,
and the last one follows from (
81
).
∎

7
The vertex algebra associated to a conformal net

In this section, starting with a conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, we construct a vertex algebra structure on the space
V=H_{0}^{f.e.}
of finite energy vectors of the vacuum sector of the conformal net. Our only assumptions on the conformal net are that it is diffeomorphism covariant, and that the generator
L_{0}
of rotations has finite-dimensional eigenspaces when acting on
H_{0}
(see Remark
7.5
for what we believe can be achieved in the absence of that second condition).

7.1
Completion of the state space

Let
D\subset{\mathbb{C}}
be a disc.
In the previous section, we have seen how to assign a vector

|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D).

to a configuration of points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{D}
, and a collection of vectors
v_{i}\in V
.
Moreover, if
D^{\prime}\subset D
is such that
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathring{D}^{\prime}
, and
v_{m+1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathring{D}\setminus D^{\prime}
, then letting
A:=D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime}
, we have

|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{D}=A[v_{m+1}(z_{m+1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})]|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\rangle_{D^{\prime}}
.

We now wish to introduce a space
\widehat{V}
which contains all the expressions
|v_{1}(z_{1})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle
, without the constraint that the points
z_{i}
lie in some specified disc.
If
V
has the weight space decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V(n)
, then we set

\widehat{V}:=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V(n)
, and call it the
algebraic completion
of
V
.

Consider the collection of all discs (not necessarily round) embedded in the complex plane.
This is a poset under inclusion, and for any conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
, there is a functor from that poset to the category of Hilbert spaces given by
D\mapsto H_{0}(D)
.
We denote the colimit by

\widehat{H_{0}}({\mathbb{C}}):=\underrightarrow{\lim}_{D}H_{0}(D).

(82)

We claim that
\widehat{H_{0}}({\mathbb{C}})
is naturally a subspace of
\widehat{V}
.
Indeed, the limit in (
82
) may be computed over the sub-poset of round discs centered at the origin. For
D
such a round disc, the Hilbert spaces
H_{0}(D)
admits a direct sum decomposition in terms of its weight spaces for rotation:

H_{0}(D)=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\!\!\!{\phantom{\big|}}^{\ell^{2}}H_{0}(D)(n).

The inclusion
V\hookrightarrow H_{0}(D)
induces isomorphisms of weight spaces
V(n)\cong H_{0}(D)(n)
, and the
\ell^{2}
-direct sum maps to the product in the obvious way:

\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\!\!\!{\phantom{\big|}}^{\ell^{2}}H_{0}(D)(n)\to\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}H_{0}(D)(n)\cong\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V(n).

(83)

Taken together, the maps (
83
) assemble to a map

\widehat{H_{0}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\widehat{V}
.
In particular, for every disc
D\subset{\mathbb{C}}
(not necessarily round) there is a canonical map
H_{0}(D)\to\widehat{V}
.

By construction, for any discs
0\in D^{\prime}\subset D\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, we then have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(D^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{D\setminus\mathring{D}^{\prime}}}

Finally, if
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
are distinct points, and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
are vectors in
V
, we write

|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle\in\widehat{V}

(84)

for the image of
|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle_{D}\in H_{0}(D)
under the map
H_{0}(D)\to\widehat{V}
, for any large enough disc
D\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.

7.2
Geometric vertex algebras

In this section we will show that the functions
|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle
constructed in (
84
) equip the vector space
V
with the structure of a
geometric vertex algebra
.
We use a definition from
[
Bru12
]
.

Definition 7.1

(Geometric vertex algebra)
.

The data of a (bounded below, locally finite dimensional) geometric vertex algebra is:

•

A
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded vector space
V=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V(n)
over
{\mathbb{C}}
with finite-dimensional weight spaces.

•

Let
\Conf_{n}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus\Delta
denote the configuration space of
n
points in
{\mathbb{C}}
.
For every
n\geq 0
,
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\Conf_{n}
and
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in V^{n}
, we have a vector

|v(z)\rangle=|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle\in\widehat{V}:=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V(n)

which is multilinear in
v
