and depends holomorphically on
z
.

This data must satisfy the following axioms.

•

(permutation invariance) For every permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
we have
|v^{\sigma}(z^{\sigma})\rangle=|v(z)\rangle
.

•

(insertion at zero) For every
v\in V
we have
|v(0)\rangle=v
.

•

(associativity) For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
p_{n}:\widehat{V}\to V(n)
denote the projection.
For all
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
,
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in V
and configurations
z\in\Conf_{m+1}
and
w\in\Conf_{n}
with
\max_{i}\left|w_{i}\right|<\min_{1\leq j\leq m}\left|z_{j}-z_{m+1}\right|
we require that the sum

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\Big\rangle

(85)

converges in the sense that, for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the
\ell
-th graded piece of the above expression is a locally uniformly absolutely convergent sum of functions of the
z_{i}
and
w_{j}
with values in
V(\ell)
.
Finally, we require that

\displaystyle\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots

\displaystyle u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\Big\rangle

where the right-hand side is defined by the sum (
85
).

•

(
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-covariance) For all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
,
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
, and
w\in\Conf_{n}
,

z.|v_{1}(w_{1})\ldots v_{n}(w_{n})\rangle=|z.v_{1}(zw_{1})\ldots z.v_{n}(zw_{n})\rangle

(86)

where
z
acts on
\widehat{V}
and
V
via multiplication by
z^{\ell}
on
V(\ell)
.

Remark 7.2
.

The definition in
[
Bru12
]
includes a meromorphicity axiom requiring that for some
N
the function
z^{N}|v(z)u(0)\rangle
has a removable singularity at
z=0
.
However, by Prop. 2.3 of loc. cit., this axiom is redundant if the grading on
V
is bounded below, which we have assumed.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net, and let

V=H_{0}^{f.e.}
be the space of finite energy vectors of its vacuum sector (Definition
6.2
).
Then the linear maps

\displaystyle V^{\otimes n}\,\,\,

\displaystyle\to\,\,\,\,\,\widehat{V}

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}\,

\displaystyle\mapsto\,|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle

defined in (
84
)
equip
V
with the structure of a geometric vertex algebra.

Proof.

The quantity
|v_{1}(z_{1})v_{2}(z_{2})\cdots v_{n}(z_{n})\rangle
depends holomorphically on the points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
by Lemma
6.13

The permutation axiom hold true by construction.
In Proposition
5.2
, we proved that worm-insertions are independent of the choice of ordering of the insertions. This independence of the choice of ordering propagates to
|v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}\rangle_{D}
, and hence to (
84
).

To show the insertion at zero axiom,
write
v=x_{1}\Omega+x_{2}\Omega
as in (
66
).
Then using the embeddings

V\hookrightarrow H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\hookrightarrow\widehat{V}
,
we have

|v(0)\rangle=|v(0)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=x_{1}\Omega+x_{2}\Omega=v
.

For
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-covariance (
86
),
it suffices to show

p_{k}\,z.|v_{1}(w_{1})\ldots v_{n}(w_{n})\rangle=p_{k}|z.v_{1}(zw_{1})\ldots z.v_{n}(zw_{n})\rangle.

(87)

Both sides are holomorphic functions of
z
and
w_{j}
,
so it’s enough to verify the equality when
\left|z\right|=1
and
\left|w_{j}\right|<1
.
In that case, by summing over
k
, (
87
) is equivalent to the equation

z.|v_{1}(w_{1})\ldots v_{n}(w_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\,=\,|z.v_{1}(zw_{1})\ldots z.v_{n}(zw_{n})\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}

in
H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
.
Let
U_{z}:H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
be the unitary induced by the automorphism
w\mapsto zw
of
{\mathbb{D}}
, and let us denote by the same letter its restriction to
V
. Then, by Lemma
6.12
, we have

\displaystyle z.|v_{1}(w_{1})\ldots v_{n}(w_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}

\displaystyle=U_{z}|v_{1}(w_{1})\ldots v_{n}(w_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}

\displaystyle=|U_{z}v_{1}(zw_{1})\ldots U_{z}v_{n}(zw_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=|z.v_{1}(zw_{1})\ldots z.v_{n}(zw_{n})\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

For the associativity axiom,
let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},

u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in V
,
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m+1},

w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}

as in the statement of the axiom.
We must show that the sum

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\,p_{\ell}\,\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle

is locally uniformly absolutely convergent, and that this sum is equal to

p_{\ell}\,\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\big\rangle.

Let
D
be a round disc centered around
z_{m+1}
, containing the points
z_{m+1}+w_{i}
in its interior, and containing none of the points
z_{j}
for
j\leq m
, and let
D^{\prime}
be a round disc centered around
0
, containing
D
and all the
z_{j}
in its interior.
It suffices to show that, in
H_{0}(D^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\;

\displaystyle p_{\ell}\,\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

(88)

\displaystyle=\,p_{\ell}\,\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

with locally uniform absolute convergence.
Let
D_{0}:=D-z_{m+1}
, and let

f:D_{0}\to D
be the isomorphism given by translation by
z_{m+1}
.
If we write
U:H_{0}(D_{0})\to H_{0}(D)
for the unitary corresponding to
f
,
then we have

\displaystyle\big|p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D}

\displaystyle=U\big|p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(0)\big\rangle_{D_{0}}

\displaystyle=Up_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle_{D_{0}}

(89)

\displaystyle=(Up_{k}U^{*})|u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}.

Let
\tilde{p}_{k}=Up_{k}U^{*}
, and note that
\sum\tilde{p}_{k}=1_{H_{0}(D)}
in the strong operator topology.
Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\;\big|p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\;\tilde{p}_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}

\displaystyle=|u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}.

Let
A:=D^{\prime}\setminus\mathring{D}
. Applying the bounded operator
A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})]
to the above equation yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\;

\displaystyle\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}},

with pointwise convergence in a neighbourhood of the
z_{j}
and
w_{i}
.

It remains to establish the locally uniform absolute convergence of (
88
).
Applying the operator
A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})]
to (
89
) gives us

\displaystyle\big|

\displaystyle v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})]\tilde{p}_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}.

By Lemma
6.13
,
A[v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})]
and
|u_{1}(w_{1}+z_{m+1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n}+z_{m+1})\rangle_{D}
depend holomorphically on the points
z_{j}
and
w_{i}
. So, on sufficiently small neighbourhoods of these points, we have

\left\|\,\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}\right\|\leq C

for some constant
C
independent of
k
and of the points
z_{j}
and
w_{i}
.
Pick a number
r<1
which close enough to
1
so that points
rz_{j}
still lie in these neighbourhoods.
By
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-covariance,

\displaystyle p_{\ell}\,

\displaystyle\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=r^{-\ell}\,p_{\ell}\,r.\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=r^{-\ell+\sum n_{j}+k}\,p_{\ell}\,\big|v_{1}(rz_{1})\ldots v_{m}(rz_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(rz_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}},

when
v_{j}\in V(n_{j})
.
Hence, on sufficiently small neighbourhoods of the
z_{j}
and
w_{i}
, we have

\left\|p_{\ell}\big|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\,p_{k}|u_{1}(w_{1})\ldots u_{n}(w_{n})\rangle(z_{m+1})\big\rangle_{D^{\prime}}\right\|\leq r^{k}C^{\prime},

where again
C^{\prime}
does not depend on
k
or the points
z_{j}
or
w_{i}
.
It follows that (
88
) converges locally uniformly absolutely, as required.
∎

Combined with
[
Bru12
, Thm 1.11]
(which states that the structures of a vertex algebra and that of a geometric vertex algebra are equivalent), the above theorem proves that
V
is naturally equipped with the structure of a vertex algebra:

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net with vacuum sector
H_{0}
, and let
V\subset H_{0}
be the subspace of finite energy vectors, equipped with its standard
{\mathbb{N}}
-grading.
Then the maps

Y(v,z)u:=|v(z)u(0)\rangle,\qquad Tv:=\frac{d}{dz}|v(z)\rangle\Big|_{z=0}

equip
V
with the structure of an
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded vertex algebra.
Moreover, whenever
\left|z_{1}\right|>\cdots>\left|z_{m}\right|
, we have

Y(v_{1},z_{1})\ldots Y(v_{m},z_{m})\Omega\,=\,|v_{1}(z_{1})\ldots v_{m}(z_{m})\rangle

(90)

as vectors in
\widehat{V}
.

Remark 7.5
.

In our definition of conformal net (Definition
2.2
),
we have chosen to include the requirement that the operator
L_{0}
has finite dimensional eigenspaces — see the footnote on page
7
for a discussion of that condition. This ensures that the graded pieces of the associated vertex algebra are finite dimensional, and is used crucially in the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

If the
L_{0}
-eigenspaces of
H_{0}
are not finite dimensional, we believe that it is still possible to get a vertex algebra structure on a suitable subspace
V=\bigoplus V(n)\subset\bigoplus H_{0}(n)=H_{0}^{f.e.}
of the space of finite energy vectors (albeit not one whose graded pieces are finite-dimensional).
The graded pieces
V(n)
consist of those vectors
\xi\in H(n)
that can be written as

\xi=\int_{M}|x_{1}(m)\ldots x_{n}(m)\rangle_{\mathbb{D}}\,d\mu(m)

where
M
is a topological space (or maybe just a manifold),
x_{i}(m)
are as in (
59
), and
\mu
is a measure on
M
.

Recall the standard vertex algebra notation
Y(u,z)v=\sum u_{(n)}v\,z^{-n-1}
(see Section
3
), so that

u_{(n)}v\,=\,\oint_{\left|z\right|=\epsilon}Y(u,z)v\,z^{n}\,dz

(91)

in
\widehat{V}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net,
let
V\subset H_{0}
be as in Corollary
7.4
, and let
u,v\in V
.
Given an embedded annulus
A=D_{out}\setminus\mathring{D}_{in}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
,
z\in\mathring{A}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\epsilon>0
small enough,
we have an equality

\oint_{w\,:\,\left|w-z\right|=\epsilon}{A}[v(z)u(w)](w-z)^{n}\,dw={A}[\big(u_{(n)}v\big)(z)]

(92)

of operators
H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
.

If
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}
is a
z
-lift of
A
, and
K
is a representation of
{\mathcal{A}}
,
we also have

\oint_{w\,:\,\left|w-z\right|=\epsilon}\underline{A}[v(z)u(w)](w-z)^{n}\,dw=\underline{A}[\big(u_{(n)}v\big)(z)]

(93)

as operators on
K
.

Proof.

By performing a translation, we may assume without loss of generality that
z=0
.
Writing
A=BC
(respectively
A=\underline{B}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{C}
), where
B
is thin (respectively
\underline{B}
is localised in some interval) and contains all the insertion points, we are reduced to proving the statements for thin/localised annuli.
By Lemma
5.6
,
we may then assume without loss of generality that
K=H_{0}
in the statement of (
93
).
Finally, (
93
) follows from (
92
) by Lemma
6.10
. So we are reduced to proving (
92
) for thin annuli.

Let
I\subset A
be contained in the thin part of
A
. By the Reeh-Schlieder theorem, the vacuum vector
\Omega_{D_{in}}
is separating among
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
-module maps
H_{0}(D_{in})\to H_{0}(D_{out})
.
The operators in (
92
) are
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
-module maps by Lemma
6.11
. So
it’s enough to check that equation upon evaluating on
\Omega_{D_{in}}
, in which case it becomes

\oint_{\left|w\right|=\epsilon}|u(w)v(0)\rangle_{D_{out}}w^{n}\,dw=|(u_{(n)}v)(0)\rangle_{D_{out}}.

The latter is immediate from (
91
) (using (
90
)).
∎

We finish this section by identifying the translation operator of the vertex algebra constructed in Corollary
7.4
.
Recall that the
translation operator
of
V
is the operator

V\to V:v\,\mapsto\,\frac{d}{dz}|v(z)\rangle\Big|_{z=0}.

(94)

Lemma 7.7
.

The translation operator (
94
) coincides with (the restriction to
V
of) the operator
L_{-1}
on
H_{0}
constructed in Proposition
4.16
.

Proof.

For
v\in V
, we will show that
\frac{d}{dz}|v(z)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}\big|_{z=0}=L_{-1}v
.
Fix
v\in V(n)
, and fix a positive number
r<1
.
For every
z\in{\mathbb{D}}
sufficiently small, we have a univalent map
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
given by
f(w)=rw+z
.
Writing just
A_{f}:H_{0}\to H_{0}
for the operator associated to the annulus
A_{f}\in\Univ
then, by Lemma
6.12
, we have:

A_{f}v=A_{f}|v(0)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}=r^{n}|v(z)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}.

(95)

The annulus
A_{f}
has a univalent framing
h:S^{1}\times[0,1]\to A_{f}
given by

h(\theta,t)=r^{1-t}e^{i\theta}+\frac{1-r^{1-t}}{1-r}z,

with associated path (Definition
4.10
) given by:

X(t)=-\frac{h_{t}}{h_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta}=-i\ln(r)\left(1-\frac{e^{-i\theta}z}{1-r}\right)\partial_{\theta}=\ln(r)L_{0}-\frac{z\ln(r)}{1-r}L_{-1}

(and since
X(t)
does not depend on
t
, we will just write
X
below).
By definition, the operator
A_{f}
on
H_{0}
is then given by

A_{f}=\prod_{1\geq t\geq 0}\Exp(X\,dt)\;\!=\exp(X).

We can then compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}|v(z)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}\Big|_{z=0}

\displaystyle=r^{-n}\frac{d}{dz}A_{f}v\Big|_{z=0}

\displaystyle=r^{-n}\frac{d}{dz}\exp\Big(\ln(r)L_{0}-\frac{z\ln(r)}{1-r}L_{-1}\Big)v\Big|_{z=0}

\displaystyle=r^{-n}\int_{0}^{1}\left.\exp((1-s)X)\left(-\frac{\ln(r)}{1-r}L_{-1}\right)\exp(sX)v\,ds\right|_{z=0}

\displaystyle=-r^{-n}\left(\frac{\ln(r)}{1-r}\right)\int_{0}^{1}r^{(1-s)L_{0}}\,L_{-1}\,r^{sL_{0}}\,v\,ds

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\ln(r)}{1-r}\int_{0}^{1}r^{1-s}\,ds\right)L_{-1}v

\displaystyle=L_{-1}v

where the first equality holds by (
95
), and
the third one holds by

[
HT25
, Lem. 3.7]
. ∎

7.3
The conformal vector

Recall that the vacuum sector
H_{0}
of the conformal net
{\mathcal{A}}
comes equipped with a positive energy representation of
\Diff_{c}(S^{1})
, which differentiates to a representation by unbounded operators of the Virasoro algebra
\Vir_{c}
(see e.g. Proposition
4.16
or
[
CKLW18
, §3.2]
). In this section, we will show that the associated stress-energy tensor

T(z):=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}L_{n}z^{-n-2}:V\to\widehat{V}

is one of the fields of the vertex algebra constructed in Corollary
7.4
. This makes
V
into a vertex operator algebra (as opposed to a mere vertex algebra), with conformal vector
L_{-2}\Omega
.

Let
\nu:=L_{-2}\Omega
, let

\displaystyle Y(\nu,z):V

\displaystyle\,\to\,\widehat{V}\qquad\qquad\text{(for $z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}$)}\hskip-56.9055pt

\displaystyle u\,\mapsto

\displaystyle\,\,|\nu(z)u(0)\rangle

be the corresponding vertex algebra field,
and let
S_{n}:V\to V
be the operators defined by

Y(\nu,z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}S_{n}z^{-n-2}.

The main goal of this section is to show that
S_{n}=L_{n}
(where the
L_{n}
’s are as in Proposition
4.16
).
By construction,
S_{-2}\Omega=\nu=L_{-2}\Omega
.
It follows by induction that

S_{-n}\Omega=L_{-n}\Omega\qquad\qquad\forall n\geq 2.\hskip-56.9055pt

(96)

Indeed, assuming
(
96
) for a given
n
,
we have

L_{-(n+1)}\Omega=\tfrac{1}{n-1}[L_{-1},L_{-n}]\Omega=\tfrac{1}{n-1}[L_{-1},S_{-n}]\Omega=S_{-(n+1)}\Omega,

where the first equality is one of the Virasoro relations, the second equality is our inductive hypothesis, and the third one is the translation axiom for the vertex algebra
V
(along with Lemma
7.7
).
We also have
L_{n}\Omega=S_{n}\Omega
for all
n\geq-1
, as both expressions are zero (the first one is zero by the classification of unitary representations of the Virasoro algebra, and the second one is zero by the vacuum axiom of the vertex algebra
V
). It follows that

Y(\nu,z)\Omega\,=\,T(z)\Omega

(97)

in
\widehat{V}
, for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

The challenge in showing
Y(\nu,z)=T(z)
is that these two objects have different technical characteristics, owing to their very different constructions.
We will geometrically modify them, so as to enable a direct comparison.

For
f\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
, let
Y(\nu,f):V\to\widehat{V}
be the
smeared field
defined by

Y(\nu,f)\,v\,:=\,\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\hat{f}(n)S_{n}v=\int_{S^{1}}f(z)\cdot z^{2}Y(\nu,z)v\,\frac{dz}{2\pi iz},

where
\hat{f}(n)
denotes the
n
-th Fourier coefficient of
f
.
We write
T(f)
for the closure of the map
V\to H_{0}:v\mapsto\sum\hat{f}(n)L_{n}v
, and recall from Proposition
4.16
that
T(f)
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}(I)
whenever
\supp(f)\subset I
.
We wish to show that
Y(\nu,f)v=T(f)v
for all
v\in V
, and
f\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
r^{\ell_{0}}
denote the round annulus
A_{r}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:r\leq|z|\leq 1\}
with its standard boundary parametrizations, and
let

\Ann^{\leq r^{\ell_{0}}}:=\{A\in\Ann\mid\exists B\in\Ann:r^{\ell_{0}}=AB\}

be the set of annuli
A
such that
r^{\ell_{0}}=AB
for some annulus
B
.

Annuli
A\in\Ann^{\leq r^{\ell_{0}}}
admit a standard embedding in the unit disc because
A_{r}\subset{\mathbb{D}}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
K
be a positive energy representation of the Virasoro algebra,
and let
K^{f.e.}\subset K
be its subspace of finite energy vectors (as in Definition
2.4
).

If
\underline{A}
and
\underline{B}\in\tAnn_{c}
are such that
\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}=r^{L_{0}}
,
then the range of
\underline{A}:K\to K
contains
K^{f.e.}
, and

\underline{A}^{-1}K^{f.e.}=\underline{B}K^{f.e.}.

(98)

Moerover,
\underline{A}^{-1}K^{f.e.}
is dense in
K
.

Proof.

Since
\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}=r^{L_{0}}
, the range of
\underline{A}
contains
K^{f.e.}
.
The inclusion
\underline{B}K^{f.e.}\subseteq\underline{A}^{-1}K^{f.e.}
is immediate, so we focus on the reverse inclusion.
If
\eta\in K
and
\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\eta\in K^{f.e.}
,
then

\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\eta=\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}\hskip 1.42262ptr^{-L_{0}}\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\eta.

By Lemma
4.15
,
\underline{A}
is injective, hence
\eta=\underline{B}r^{-L_{0}}\underline{A}\eta
. It follows that
\eta\in\underline{B}K^{f.e.}
, as required.

Finally, since
K^{f.e.}
is dense in
K
, and
\underline{B}:K\to K
has dense image (Lemma
4.15
), the subspace (
98
) is also dense in
K
.
∎

Given an annulus
A
, we denote by

\partial_{out}^{th}A:=\partial_{out}A\setminus(\partial_{out}A\cap\partial_{in}A)

the “thick part” of the outgoing boundary of
A
.
If
A\in\Ann^{\leq r^{\ell_{0}}}
, then
using the standard embedding of
A
into
{\mathbb{D}}
, we may view
\partial_{out}^{th}A
as a subset of
S^{1}
.
Let
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
be a lift
of
A
, and let
f\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
be supported in some interval
I\subset\partial_{out}^{th}A
.
Then the operator

\underline{A}^{*}\hskip 1.42262ptT(f)\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{A}

on
H_{0}
is densely defined by Lemma
7.9
, and can be used to define
a sesquilinear form

\xi,\eta\,\mapsto\,\langle\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle T(f)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle

with domain
\underline{A}^{-1}V
.

Now consider the sesquilinear form

\xi,\eta\,\mapsto\,\langle Y(\nu,f)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle
,
which is a smeared version of the sesquilinear form

\xi,\eta\,\mapsto\,\langle Y(\nu,z)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle

with same domain
\underline{A}^{-1}V
.
The latter can be understood as coming from a point insertion in the doubled annulus
\hat{A}=A^{\dagger}\cup A
:

Lemma 7.10
.

Fix
A\in\Ann^{\leq r^{\ell_{0}}}
, with its standard embedding in
{\mathbb{D}}
, along with a lift
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
.
Let
\underline{A}^{\dagger}\in\Ann_{c}
be the conjugate of
\underline{A}
, with underlying annulus
A^{\dagger}
, realized in
{\mathbb{C}}
as the image of
A
under the reflection
z\mapsto\overline{z}^{-1}
.
Finally let
\underline{\hat{A}}:=\underline{A}^{\dagger}\underline{A}
, with underlying annulus
\hat{A}:=A^{\dagger}\cup A\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.

Then, for any
\xi,\eta\in\underline{A}^{-1}V
,
v\in V
, and
z\in\mathring{\hat{A}}
, we have

\langle\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle Y(v,z)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle.

(99)

Proof.

If
A=A_{r}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:r\leq|z|\leq 1\}
, then (
99
) is equivalent to

\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]u=r^{L_{0}}Y(v,z)r^{L_{0}}u\vskip-2.84526pt

(100)

for
u\in V
and
z\in\mathring{\hat{A}}
.
Both expressions being holomorphic, it is enough to show (
100
) for
\left|z\right|<1
,
in which case
\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]=r^{L_{0}}\underline{A}_{r}[v(z)]
.
So we are reduced to showing
\underline{A}_{r}[v(z)]u=Y(v,z)r^{L_{0}}u
in
\widehat{V}
.
Unpacking the definitions, this is equivalent to showing the equality

\underline{A}_{r}[v(z)]u=|v(z)(r^{L_{0}}u)(0)\rangle_{{\mathbb{D}}}

in
H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
.
The latter is an instance of Lemma
6.12
.

Back to the general case, choose
\underline{B}\in\tAnn_{c}
such that
\underline{A}_{r}=\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}
.
We then have
\hat{A}_{r}=\underline{B}^{\dagger}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\hat{A}}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}
.
If
\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\in V
then,
by Lemma
7.9
, we may write
\xi=\underline{B}\xi^{\prime}
and
\eta=\underline{B}\eta^{\prime}
.
We can then compute:

\displaystyle\langle\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]\xi,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]\underline{B}\xi^{\prime},\underline{B}\eta^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\underline{B}^{*}\underline{\hat{A}}[v(z)]\underline{B}\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\underline{\hat{A}}_{r}[v(z)]\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Y(v,z)r^{L_{0}}\xi^{\prime},r^{L_{0}}\eta^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Y(v,z)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle.\qed

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net, let
V
be the associated vertex algebra (constructed in Corollary
7.4
),
and let
Y:V\to\End(V)[[z^{\pm 1}]]
be the be state-field correspondence of the vertex algebra.

Let
T(z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}L_{n}z^{-n-2}
be the stress-energy tensor of
{\mathcal{A}}
(constructed in Proposition
4.16
),
and let
\nu:=L_{-2}\Omega
.
Then

Y(\nu,z)=T(z),

and
V
is a vertex operator algebra with conformal vector
\nu
.

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
be supported in some interval
I
.
Fix
J
containing
I
in its interior, and let
\underline{A}\in\tAnn_{c}(J)\cap\Ann^{\leq r^{\ell_{0}}}
be localised in
J
, with
I\subset\partial_{out}^{th}A
.
Finally, let
\underline{\hat{A}}:=\underline{A}^{\dagger}\underline{A}
, with underlying annulus
\hat{A}
embedded in
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
as in Lemma
7.10
.

By

Lemmas
6.13
and
6.8
, the assignment
z\mapsto\underline{A}[\nu(z)]

defines a holomorphic function
\mathring{\hat{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}(J)
.
Since
I\subset\mathring{\hat{A}}
,
the expression

\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]:=\int_{S^{1}}f(z)z^{2}\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu(z)]\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}

(101)

makes sense, and is again valued in
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
.

By Lemma
7.10
,
the sesquilinear form on
\underline{A}^{-1}V
associated to

\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]
satisfies

\langle\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle Y(\nu,f)\underline{A}\xi,\underline{A}\eta\rangle.

(102)

Pick
\underline{B}\in\Ann_{c}
so that
r^{L_{0}}=\underline{A}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{B}

and set
\xi_{0}:=\underline{B}\Omega\in\underline{A}^{-1}V
, so as to have
\underline{A}\xi_{0}=\Omega
.
By (
97
), we then have

\langle\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]\xi_{0},\eta\rangle=\langle Y(\nu,f)\Omega,\underline{A}\eta\rangle=\langle T(f)\Omega,\underline{A}\eta\rangle=\langle\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}\xi_{0},\eta\rangle

for all
\eta\in\underline{A}^{-1}V
.
As
\underline{A}^{-1}V
is dense in
H_{0}
, it follows that

\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]\xi_{0}=\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}\xi_{0}.

The product
\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}
is densely defined by Lemma
7.9
.
Since
\underline{A}\in{\mathcal{A}}(J)
, and since
T(f)
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
(Proposition
4.16
),
the closure of
\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}
is also affiliated with
A(J)
.
Recall that we also have
\hat{A}[\nu[f]]\in{\mathcal{A}}(J)
by (
101
).

The vector
\xi_{0}
is separating for
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
by the lemma below. It follows that, upon taking closures, we have
\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}=\underline{\hat{A}}[\nu[f]]
.
By (
102
), the expressions

\underline{A}^{*}T(f)\underline{A}
and
\underline{A}^{*}Y(\nu,f)\underline{A}
thus agree as sesquilinear form on
\underline{A}^{-1}V
,
and therefore

T(f)u=Y(\nu,f)u

(103)

for every
u\in V
(this is a priori an equality of vectors in
\widehat{V}
, but the energy bounds for
T(f)
ensure that this vector lies in fact in
H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
).
The function
f
in (
103
) was initially required to be supported in some interval
I
; using a partition of unity we have the same identity for every
f\in C^{\infty}(S^{1})
.
Setting
f(z)=z^{n}
, we see that the modes of
T(z)
and
Y(\nu,z)
coincide, as desired:

\qquad\qquad S_{n}=L_{n},\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

By Proposition
4.16
, the modes of
T(z)
satisfy the Virasoro relations.
The same therefore holds for the modes
S_{n}
of
Y(\nu,z)
.
By definition,
L_{0}
is the grading operator for
V
, the same therefore holds for the zero-mode
S_{0}
of
Y(\nu,z)
.
Finally,
Y(S_{-1}v,z)=Y(L_{-1}v,z)=\frac{d}{dz}Y(v,z)
by Lemma
7.7
.
This finished the proof that
\nu=L_{-2}\Omega
is a conformal vector for
V
.
∎

We conclude this section with a lemma that was used in the above proof.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
J\subset S^{1}
be an interval,
let
\underline{A}\in\Ann_{c}
, and let
\xi_{0}:=\underline{A}\Omega\in H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
.
Then
\xi_{0}
is a separating vector for the action of
{\mathcal{A}}(J)
on
H_{0}
.

Proof.

Applying the Riemann uniformization theorem to the disc
A\cup{\mathbb{D}}
, we may write
\underline{A}=\underline{\gamma}\underline{B}
, for some
\underline{B}\in\Univ
, and
\underline{\gamma}
a lift of some diffeomorphism
\gamma\in\Diff(S^{1})
.
By (
25
), we then have
\xi_{0}=\underline{A}\Omega=\underline{\gamma}\Omega
.

If
x\xi_{0}=0
for some
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(J)
, then

\big(\underline{\gamma}^{-1}x\underline{\gamma}\big)\Omega=\underline{\gamma}^{-1}x\xi_{0}=0.

As
\underline{\gamma}^{-1}x\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\gamma^{-1}(J))
, it follows from the Reeh-Schlieder theorem that

\underline{\gamma}^{-1}x\underline{\gamma}=0
,
and hence that
x=0
as well.
∎

7.4
Unitarity of the vertex algebra associated to a conformal net

We have shown that the vector space
V:=H_{0}^{f.e.}\subset H_{0}
of finite energy vectors inside the vacuum sector of a conformal net
is a vertex operator algebra.
Its state-field correspondence is given by
Y(a,z)b=|a(z)b(0)\rangle
,
and its conformal net is
\nu=L_{-2}\Omega
. Our next goal is to equip
V
with the structure of a unitary vertex operator algebra.

Recall (Definition
3.3
) that an inner product
\langle\,\,,\,\rangle
and an involution
\Theta
on a vertex operator algebra
V
equip it with the structure of a
unitary vertex operator algebra
if:

1.
\Theta
is an anti-linear vertex operator algebra automorphism. Namely, it fixes
\Omega
and
\nu
, and satisfies

\Theta Y(a,z)\Theta=Y(\Theta a,\overline{z})
.
2. The following equation holds:

\langle a,Y(u,z)b\rangle=\langle Y(e^{\bar{z}L_{1}}(-\bar{z}^{-2})^{L_{0}}\Theta u,\bar{z}^{-1})a,b\rangle
.

In our notations, the first condition becomes:

\Theta|a(z)b(0)\rangle=|(\Theta a)(\bar{z})(\Theta b)(0)\rangle,

(104)

and the second one is (see Remark
3.5
):

\displaystyle\big\langle a,|u(z)b(0)\rangle\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle|(\Theta u)(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto(\bar{z}+w)^{-1})a(0)\rangle,b\big\rangle

(105)

\displaystyle=\big\langle|u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1})a(0)\rangle,b\big\rangle,

where the last expression uses the anti-holomorphic local coordinates explained in Section
6.3
.
Note that the sesquilinear pairing
\langle\,\,,\,\rangle:V\times V\to{\mathbb{C}}
is extended to a pairing

V\times\widehat{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}
in the left hand side of (
105
), and to a pairing

\widehat{V}\times V\to{\mathbb{C}}
in the right hand side of (
105
).

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a conformal net, and let
V
be the associated vertex operator algebra.
Then the inner product on
V
inherited from
H_{0}
together with the involution
\Theta:V\to V
(introduced in (
34
) and further discussed in Section
6.3
) equip
V
with the structure of a unitary vertex operator algebra.

Proof.

We first show that
\Theta
is a vertex operator algebra automorphism.
It fixes the vacuum by construction.
To see that it fixes the conformal vector
\nu
, recall from (
35
) that
\Theta L_{-2}\Theta=L_{-2}
.
It follows that

\Theta\nu=\Theta L_{-2}\Omega=\Theta L_{-2}\Theta\Omega=L_{-2}\Omega=\nu.

Finally,
\Theta
satisfies condition (
104
) by Lemma
6.16
, and is therefore a vertex operator algebra automorphism.

To complete the proof, we must verify (
105
).
Pick
r
such that
0<r<\min(|z|,|z^{-1}|)
.
Let
D
be the disc of radius
r
,

D^{\prime}
the disc of radius
r^{-1}
, and
A:=D^{\prime}\setminus\mathring{D}
, with standard lift
\underline{A}
.
The map
w\mapsto\bar{w}^{-1}
induces an isomorphism
A^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A
.
By Proposition
6.19
, the adjoint of
\underline{A}[u(z)]
is therefore given by

\underline{A}[u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1}]
.
Letting
U:H_{0}(D)\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
and
U^{\prime}:H_{0}(D^{\prime})\to H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})
be the unitaries associated to the map
w\mapsto r^{-1}w
and
w\mapsto rw
, respectively.
We then compute:

\displaystyle\big\langle a,|u(z)b(0)\rangle_{\widehat{V}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle r^{2L_{0}}a,|u(z)b(0)\rangle_{D^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle r^{2L_{0}}a,A[u(z)]|b(0)\rangle_{D}\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle r^{2L_{0}}a,U^{\prime*}\underline{A}[u(z)]U|b(0)\rangle_{D}\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\underline{A}[u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1}]U^{\prime}|r^{2L_{0}}a(0)\rangle_{D^{\prime}},U|b(0)\rangle_{D}\big\rangle_{H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\underline{A}[u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1}]U|a(0)\rangle_{D^{\prime}},U^{\prime}|r^{2L_{0}}b(0)\rangle_{D}\big\rangle_{H_{0}({\mathbb{D}})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle U^{\prime*}\underline{A}[u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1}]U|a(0)\rangle_{D},r^{2L_{0}}b\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle{A}[u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1}]|a(0)\rangle_{D},r^{2L_{0}}b\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle|u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1})a(0)\rangle_{D^{\prime}},r^{2L_{0}}b\big\rangle_{H_{0}(D^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\big\langle|u(\bar{z}^{-1};w\mapsto\overline{(z+w)}^{-1})a(0)\rangle_{\widehat{V}},b\big\rangle,

where the third and seventh equalities hold by Lemma
6.10
.
∎
